# utxo-partial-order: design notes

## State of the series, and how to read this document

The series is one lineage of changes under one name, `utxo-partial-order`. It has been cut three times, and every cut is the same design:

|---|---|---|

| The machines port | the same design with master's guarantees restated as state machines, published copies and named preconditions; first 32 commits on master a527a0077a, tip 18dd95aeb5, then reviewed and extended | earlier states of branch `utxo-partial-order` |

At 82bda590a7: built without a warning, unit suite 403 passed and one skipped, 17 functional runs pass. Not tested and named so: a disk that fills between claim and write; a read-ahead that fails; a target that changes between the reorganisation's holds; the read use of `InvalidateBlock()`. No slow-disk measurement exists for any of the file work. The full functional suite and dbcrash last ran on fb1255d0f0, before all of it. Before it: 34 commits on master 19128e8b77, tip 85197a850d since 2026-11-23, on the maintainer's go ("Ok, execute.") for the judgement from the block store machine's preconditions: commit 34 new (85197a850d, "blockstorage, validation: file space, trims and pruned files are handled off cs_main"); commits 32 and 33 unchanged; the block write is not queued behind the connect (asked by the maintainer, then withdrawn: "No, don't do that"; O2 and O11 rest on the write being done before the block is recorded as stored; do not propose again). The goal is meant strictly (the maintainer: "Yes, it is meant strictly."), and the rule is written in `validation.h` under "File I/O", after O15: file work is made between two states of the machine with cs_main free, so that nothing waits for it but what it is made for; a transition under cs_main only decides and records. Commit 34 moves the three violations that were on the download path: the claim of a position notes chunks by arithmetic (`BlockManager::ClaimChunks()`) and the write allocates under a leaf mutex of the sequence (`Preallocate()`, `FlatFileSeq::AllocateChunks()`, called from `WriteBlockAt()` and `WriteBlockUndoAt()`); both trims at a file switch are queued on the effects executor, the block file's once no write is in flight for it (`m_blockfiles_to_trim`, `UnpinBlockFile()`); automatic pruning removes its files on a block I/O thread, `pruneblockchain` still inline. Left under cs_main on purpose and named in the rule: the syncs of a forced flush, the reads of `DisconnectTip()`, the external loader, loading and verifying at the start; tip and RPC writes stay on their callers (the maintainer: "little point in optimizing those"). Behaviour changed: a full disk is found by the write, not the claim (same fatal error, the write fails); `FindNextBlockPos()` no longer returns a null position and `FindUndoPos()` no longer fails, the callers' checks at blockstorage.cpp 1233 and 1514 and validation.cpp 6430 are unreachable and were left in place; automatically pruned files go shortly after the flush, not inside it. "The series" in this document means this lineage. Where a passage gives a tip, a number of commits or a backup branch, it is the state at the time of that passage, not today's; today's state is the last row above and nothing else. Reading rules:

- A commit cited by number alone is a position in a list of the first cut: the list in 9.3, or the numbering of the time of the passage where commits had been folded or dropped since. "Commit N of the machines series" is a position in a state of the port before its consolidation. Neither is a position in 9.4; a commit of the current cut is cited by its subject.
- Sections 3 and 4.1 to 4.10 describe the current cut: they were re-read against the code at 87d0a6a1a6 on 2026-11-21 and corrected where the port or the current cut changed the mechanism. Their figures were taken on the first cut unless the passage names another, and a commit number in them is still a position in the first cut's list. Sections 1, 2 and 4.11 stand as written for the first cut. 4.12 is on the current cut, and so is 4.14 (file I/O, written against b5fc79e546 on 2026-11-23). What changed in the store on 2026-11-22 (the slim change record, the record of plain bytes, the changes applied from the view's entries, the plain shard mutex) is entered in 4.2 in short; the account of each is in 5.2b and 5.2.
- The measurements of section 7 were taken on the first cut, except the rows that name the machines series and sections 7.9 and 7.10, which are on the current cut's tree.
- The validation record of 9.2 and 9.3 is the first cut's. For the port: its stages and gates are in the safety plan, its review rounds in the review plan; a ThreadSanitizer pass was made on the port on 2026-11-17, before the commits that came after it. On the current cut: review rounds 13 (21 November) and 14 (22 November, read only, its fixes folded the same day), a ThreadSanitizer pass at 3c224b4d8b on 21 November and none since, the maintainer's `feature_dbcrash.py` run at 87d0a6a1a6, and his full functional suite and `feature_dbcrash.py` on fb1255d0f0 on 22 November, the last run of either; the record is in 5.2a, 5.3 and 5.5. The production code changed since fb1255d0f0 by the plain shard mutex, by the rebase onto 19128e8b77 (77 upstream commits, two conflict resolutions) and by commit 32 (block reads on I/O threads).

This document is the design record. The working records it points to are local files and are not part of it:

- The port's plan and record: `/work/utxo-partial-order-safety-plan.md`. Its review: `/work/parallel-validation/review-plan-full-series.md` with the round files beside it, and `performance-suggestions.md`. The consolidation into the current cut: `/work/parallel-validation/consolidation-plan.md`.
- Patches: `/work/patches/utxo-partial-order.mbox` is the current cut, `utxo-partial-order-v1.mbox` the first. The trial and probe patch files, the probe scripts and data, the first cut's review reports and the raw reader output of the port's rounds were removed on 2026-11-18: their results are recorded in this document and in the kept records, and the trial branches named in the text were deleted on 2026-11-22 (5.6).
- The harness: `/work/utxo-partial-order-harness`, with chain D kept in `chains/chainD`.
- Worktree: `/work/bitcoin/.claude/worktrees/parallel-validation-machines` holds the current cut (the directory kept the port's name). The first cut has no worktree and, since 2026-11-22, no branch; it is the mbox named above.
- Branches today (2026-11-22): `utxo-partial-order` and `utxo-partial-order-baseline`, nothing else (5.6, 9.4). Branches kept on 2026-11-18, for the record: `utxo-partial-order`, `utxo-partial-order-v1`, `-before-consolidation` (the port's history before its consolidation), `-before-recut`, `-spend-hints`, `-young-table-trial`, `-span`, `-lookups`; and `-baseline` (master e211dd0186 plus the harness probe, 6.3), added later that day. Every other backup, probe and trial branch named in this document was deleted that day; such a name is a state of its time and no longer resolves. Commit ids that only those branches held resolve until the repository is garbage-collected; after that they too are labels of their time. The first cut's review record moved to a file of its own the same day (4.11).

The design document of the series: its state by topic. It replaces the dated log of 2026-11-09 to 13; the working records beside it are named at the head. Each fact stands in one place; other places point to it by section number. A new result is entered at its topic and replaces what it supersedes; no dated section is appended. The state of the series is at the head of the document; a commit is best found by its subject.

Where a statement is measured it says so; the rest is read from the code. Marks on figures: R a real sync or the maintainer's machine, H harness (direction only), I inferred, "none" never measured alone. Standing caveat: harness ratios landed at about a third on the real machine.

## 1. Goal

Three ideas went in. From sipa's post in the libbitcoin thread: a missing input is an obligation to settle later, not a failure, so applying blocks stops being a total order. From libbitcoin: validation split from confirmation, only confirmation ordered. From Hornet and the research's section 9.5: a store written by age at a steady rate, with nothing emptied, and a compact entry. The window, deferral by prefix and the status flip deliver the first two and the oracle tests pin them; the third was delivered in mechanism at first and in effect only through the work of sections 4.4 and 4.8.

Ruling of the maintainer (2026-11-13): the series exists to remove every limitation on the validation side that does not scale with more hardware, so that the p2p part can then be optimised freely. Lines of code are not a criterion against it. That the connect outruns the delivery on a real sync is the intended result; the master reindex figure is of interest but decides nothing. The yardstick for each component is whether it removes or leaves a non-scaling limit in validation.

Phases: speed work on the connect; then, on the maintainer's word, consolidation and low-resource defaults (tuning instrumentation removed); the connect work paused at the 17:39 run and the receive side taken up; the design review of 2026-11-13 (sections 2, 4.1, 5, 7.6).

The current cut (2026-11-18): the port re-cut into 22 commits on master e211dd0186 (tip 74e8e1db9a, same tree as before the re-cut; backup `utxo-partial-order-before-recut`); the list is in 9.4, the gates in `/work/parallel-validation/consolidation-plan.md` section 8.

## 2. Constraints, each with its state

The text is the plan's section 1 (stage references left out), the state is the design review's, read at 10588 lines over 72 files and carried forward to the tip.

- **Consensus results identical.** Holds. Shown by the two store oracles against an in-order reference (`random_order_window_matches_in_order`, `same_as_cache_under_random_operations`), the fuzz target `coinsstore_window`, `validation_core_tests` and the unchanged functional suite; `src/consensus` and `src/script` are untouched. The one recorded gap (BIP30, 4.6) is closed. Open: the fuzz target does not reach the shard's own table or the depot and never gives the mark flag.
- **Crash safety equal to master.** Shown in part: `feature_async_undo` aborts before the first undo write, `feature_coins_lag` aborts inside write-out runs with reorgs mixed in and warns itself that the hook may not fire. Never exercised: crash recovery under load, a crash of a pruned node.
- **C1, the four architecture goals.** Pure validation functions (the core reads only its arguments and the view: no member, global, clock, log, file or lock); pure state machines (named transitions, asserted phases); asynchronous I/O (every write and sync is an effect on an executor, never under a validation lock); a radical reduction of `cs_main` scope. Oracles: `validation_core_tests` running the core with no `TestingSetup`, the `StagedConnect` phase assertions, the thread-safety annotations on a clang build. State: partly. Purity amended 15 November (the maintainer, "fine for now"): the core reads nothing of the node's (no member, global, file or lock; policy and time through BlockValidationContext); logging is allowed in the core and the consensus functions (amended 15 November, round 11 X7/V4, the maintainer: "Logging in the consensus functions is obviously fine!"; e.g. master's LogError lines in the free DisconnectBlock), and the one other exception is master's bench timing, a steady-clock read moved with the code it times; the purity script is dropped as an oracle. "No I/O under a validation lock" has one exception left: the per-acceptance `FlushStateToDisk` under cs_main that waits for the effects thread (4.7), since commit 15 (the pruning waits) only when pruning takes files (the prune event's block-index write and unlink stay under the lock, as in master); the full write once forced before the first disconnect of a reorg is gone since 14 November (4.4).
- **C2, default architecture, no new IBD mode.** No hints, no second validation path, no per-mode configuration. The window and the lag are run-time policies of one code path: the window is one when one block is available and larger when blocks are queued; the lag follows the memory budget. `-connectwindow=1` was meant as the same code with the window pinned; in the code it takes the serial path (`use_window` requires `ConnectWindow() > 1`). Known deviation, kept on the maintainer's word (15 November, round 11 X1/W1/T1/V1): the window, the receive hand-off and the request rule are chosen by `IsInitialBlockDownload()`, against this constraint and C17; a node that falls behind after the latch catches up serially; the historical chainstate connecting serially is wanted (it leaves the pool and memory to the active chain). Candidate for later (B): the active chainstate takes the window when more than one block is ready, the historical chainstate stays serial; it needs a catch-up functional test with a live mempool, an IBD re-measurement, and C17's reading written down (its text forbids asking whether a block is the only one in flight). C (the window always, also for one tip block) belongs to 5.2a. Also accepted (15 November, round 12 X2): outside initial block download each block's undo write and completions are awaited under cs_main, as master writes undo synchronously in ConnectBlock; inside it they stay asynchronous. The wait is kept for notification order (BlockConnected and UpdatedBlockTip leave with the block, announcements by headers, a transaction announced after the block it builds on), which does not matter in initial block download; no code change. Added later by the maintainer: no further special-case rules for when the window opens. State: holds. Two new options, `-blockcache` and `-connectwindow`; `-prevoutfetchthreads` sizes the connect pool.
- **C3, the tip as fast or faster than today, with everything the tip already has.** The block at the tip is reconstructed from the mempool through compact blocks, its transactions arrive hashed, its inputs are warm, and its scripts are mostly not run because the script execution and signature caches hold the mempool's verification. Each must survive: no re-hashing of reconstructed transactions, a store whose lookups warm the coins the mempool touches, the `ValidationCache` passed into the core unchanged, parallel prevout fetching for cold inputs. At a window of one nothing is deferred and a missing coin fails as today. Oracles: a `ConnectBlock` bench on a mainnet block with prevouts in a warm store and scripts off, and a regtest bench that accepts a block's transactions into a mempool first and then connects the block; both against master. State: the tip bench through the store exists since 15 November (ConnectBlockMainnetTipStore against TipOverlay, 5.2a): the store's round trip 1.14x master's shape, Confirm() the difference; the regtest mempool bench has no figure at a recent tip.
- **C4, low memory and few cores.** IBD must work on a node with 4 GB of RAM and a system-on-chip CPU. Every in-memory structure has a budget and the budgets sum to one stated figure. (The plan's "worker default not from the core count" is superseded by one thread a core, 4.9.) State: open, see 7.6; the byte budget per structure does not exist as one table.
- **C5, ultraprune.** Nothing requires a block older than the prune depth: a pruned node prunes only below the written head (the lag cap is gone, amended 15 November, 5.7), so a replay after a crash reads only kept blocks, pruning never removes a file inside the lag, and undo writes are settled before a block file is pruned. The stored UTXO set never exceeds what a client running today's code has at the same height: what is on disk is a subset of the set at the lag's lower head, with no history beside it; the write-out may issue an erase for a coin that was never written (accepted, 5.5), which leaves nothing on disk. At shutdown the lag is drained so the stored set is exactly the tip's. No on-disk representation that keeps deleted entries past compaction. State: mechanism present (4.10). No new functional test with `-prune`; no pruned sync measured in isolation.
- **C6, optional indexes unchanged.** `BlockConnected` fires per block in height order after the block's undo data is written; the indexes see exactly what they see today. State: shown for one case (`feature_async_undo` with `-txindex` and `-coinstatsindex`).
- **C7, blocks kept in memory are bounded.** The `-blockcache` budget bounds the parsed blocks in flight; nothing holds a block past acceptance outside that budget, the window's blocks included. State: partly; blocks held by receive tasks, the window and the bins lie outside it (7.6).
- **C8, no additional thread pools; asynchrony through effects.** One pool runs the apply tasks and the prevout fetch tasks; the script check queue remains for the tip. A block waiting for lower blocks holds no thread. All I/O stays on the effects executors, except two blocking reads issued by the apply workers: database reads for old coins, and the read of a window block's own block file when the block is neither passed in nor in the block cache (`RunConnectTask()`, with the release of old block files from the page cache that follows it). State: amended on 22 November (the maintainer: I/O to the edges is a goal of the series, a pool of blocking threads is fine, as little OS-specific code as possible): the effects executor has four further threads for unordered I/O, and the read of a window block's file is made there since commit 32, no longer by the apply worker; the database reads for old coins are the one blocking I/O the workers still issue, and the received block's write still ran on a worker. Since the same day (commits 32 and 33 of the 33-commit cut) the four threads are a pool of the block manager (`RunBlockIO()`), not of the executor, and the received block's write in initial block download is made there too; the coin database reads remain the workers' one blocking I/O. Before that the block file read was added as an exception on 16 November (the maintainer, question 3 of the blind-spot assessment: moving it to the effects executor or to the connector would add a serial section; no code change); amended 15 November (the maintainer: the helper threads are fine, the short-lived flush threads not): the connect pool, the effects executor thread and the connector thread are accepted beside master's script check queue; a forced flush's further workers are tasks of the connect pool since commit 48 (claimed ranges, so a flush holding cs_main never waits for a queued task).
- **C9, optimise for mainnet.** Every measurement and bench uses mainnet data or mainnet blocks where one can be used. Empty blocks and signet are not design inputs. **C10, measure, don't guess; gains hardware-independent.** No knob is estimated. Expected gains are ratios derived from a measured share, never times on a machine class. Measurements on real data are the maintainer's to run. **C11, no time or effort estimates.** Sequencing and gates only. State of the three: process rules, kept.
- **C12, per-block fixed costs are multiplied by the block count.** Each stage states how many hand-offs per block it adds, keeps that number fixed and small, has a bench for it, and the tip path has none. State: counted 15 November from the code: a block from a peer in initial block download crosses threads about five times (handler to receive task, task to connector, connector to window task, worker's `applied` back to the connector, undo write to the effects thread with its BlockConnected riding the same queue), plus one for a continuation; a tip block three times (handler to connector, undo write and notification, the block freed by a pool task). The tip departs from "none": both tip hand-offs were measured at no cost (tip latency parity, the connector decision); the pool's wake-up has its bench (threadpool bench, with the pause as baseline).
- **C13, the flush is a symptom.** The cause is removed, the total order and the burst write-out, not the symptom. A change whose only effect is to make the flush less visible does not count toward a gate. State: amended 15 November (the maintainer): the cause is removed by the lag (runs of the oldest changes); the write path's own efficiency work (key-range shards, sorted whole-shard batches, the flush workers) is accepted on its own merits and counts toward no gate, since the full write still happens at shutdown, a cache resize and the periodic write.
- **C14, trust unchanged.** Nothing is assumed beyond assumevalid. Every consensus rule is evaluated; a deferred check is evaluated later, never skipped, and a check whose input is unknown fails rather than defaulting. State: holds.
- **C15, deliverable form.** An mbox of per-commit patches that applies to master with `git am --3way`; nothing is pushed; no code until the maintainer triggers it. The kernel library is not built during development; every new source file that belongs to it is added to `src/kernel/CMakeLists.txt` in the commit that introduces it. Commits carry no assistant trailers (the maintainer's later word, superseding the plan). State: holds; the kernel source list is complete, the kernel library never linked on this branch.
- **C16, existing tests are the oracle.** Functional tests are not changed except where an on-disk file or a log line they read has changed, each edit named in the commit message. Unit tests change only where the interface they call changed, minimally. New tests may be added freely. A design that would need an existing functional test rewritten is a finding against the design. State: six departures, accepted 15 November (the maintainer), each following from a deliberate behaviour change and none weakening its check beyond the documented notification order. Functional: `p2p_segwit` (one log text; `feature_coinstatsindex` is master's again since the forced write before a reorg went, 14 November). Unit: `chainstate_write_tests` (the subscriber reads the flushed block from the locator since the flush is asynchronous, the heaviest of the six), `validation_chainstatemanager_tests` (default and clamp value), small additions to three fuzz targets. `p2p_ibd_stalling` passes with the request rule.

Given by the maintainer after the plan, without a number:

- **The low-end write-out logic stays** (lag runs, eviction, the owed erase). Holds, with unit cases (`streamed_run_records_the_settled_head`, `evict_clean_and_bound_for_bytes`, `owed_erase_survives_a_create_and_its_unwind`).
- **Network work changes the receiving side only, no protocol change.** Holds: `net.cpp`, `net.h` and `protocol` untouched. A peer can observe two things: a ping is answered only after that peer's handed-off blocks are processed, and a slow peer is asked for fewer blocks at a time.
- **C17, performance work treats every block alike (15 November, the maintainer).** No code asks whether the chain is at the tip, or whether a block is the only one in flight, to choose a cheaper path; and a gain for connecting a single block applies to blocks connected during initial block download too. Decisions may follow from the data (how many of a block's inputs miss the tier), never from where the chain is. Rules out a solo-block confirm and an inline connect when the connector is idle; keeps an unwind driven by the block's undo data instead of a per-change record, and a prefetch sized by its misses. First step: a split timing of Confirm() and a warm-scripts tip bench.
- **No LevelDB tuning.** Holds. There is no LevelDB LOG file in the chainstate directory to ask for; the direction is the pipeline and our side of the flush.
- **Allocation failures terminate and logic errors stay uncaught.** Holds (4.5 for the pool; the broad handler around the compaction names `std::system_error`).

Never checked: the kernel library link; crash recovery under load; C3's benches; the historical chainstate under a snapshot, which gets neither window nor connector.

The four wordings once open here (C8, C13, C1's purity, C16) were ruled on 15 November (4.11). The list as yardstick was taken as accepted with "Continue".

## 3. Invariants

From the behaviour-only specification of 2026-11-09 (the specification and its 149 checks were used up by a review round and removed). Re-read against the current cut on 2026-11-21: I1 to I5, I8, I10 and I12 hold as worded; I6, I7, I9, I11 and I13 are worded here for what the code does now.

- I1 Readers under the main lock see a chain tip and a UTXO set of the same height; a block's changes appear atomically with the tip update.
- I2 Every consensus verdict (accept or reject, and the reason), the undo data and the fee total equal in-order validation's.
- I3 Before a run the database holds the state at the marker's old head; each write is the outpoint's value at the new head; replay repairs any prefix. The settled head after a run is a block whose state the database holds.
- I4 A fresh entry is never in the database; an entry collected by a run is not fresh; a spent dirty entry is kept until its erase lands; a coin created and spent inside the lag is never written. The entry machine's table (`coins_entry_machine.h`, `IsStored()`) carries this now.
- I5 Change tags: the block height for connects; the fork height for a reorg's inverse changes, except restored coins created above the settled height; every change tagged at or below a run's bound is carried by the run, or holds the settled height down.
- I6 An entry an open block created or spent (`Pending()`: flagged and above the confirmed height) is never evicted (`EvictClean` takes clean entries only) and never written as that block's change; a run writes the confirmed value under it (4.4).
- I7 Conflicts are charged to the block at fault. A window block's failure is provisional: the window drains, the block is unwound and connected alone, and that verdict decides (4.5). A block's own verdict is never altered by another block's actions on the view.
- I8 Every block above a failed or abandoned block is unwound before anything else happens; an unwound block's creates vanish and its spends are forgotten.
- I9 No pool task waits for a task of its own pool, and work that is waited for while a listed lock is held takes none of those locks (`WAITED_FOR_UNDER_LOCK`, `AssertMayWait`, `NO_WAIT_LOCK` in sync.h). Receive tasks, the one kind of pool task that takes `cs_main`, leave one worker free (`LockTaskLimit()`, 4.7).
- I10 Block index batches commit in preparation order; the marker never names a head whose block, undo and index data are not durable.
- I11 The window never makes the node accept a block it would reject alone, or the reverse. Outside IBD the verdicts are master's; the path is not: activation runs on the connector thread, a block connected alone queues its undo write before its apply and takes the coins its apply misses from its undo data, and the previous block's transactions are freed off the new block's path (the three tip commits of 9.4).
- I12 A crash at any point (inside a run, inside a block, at a reorg, at shutdown) is followed by a start-up replay that yields a UTXO set equal to that of a node that never crashed.
- I13 Memory: the store's usage is bounded by `-dbcache` as the cache's was (a write-out run's target is one half of the budget, 4.4). What lies outside it is counted in the byte ledger's accounts (block cache, window, undo backlog, pinned blocks; `util/byte_ledger.h`) or listed in 4.9.

Stated in the code since the port and not repeated in this list: the ordering preconditions O1 to O15 (validation.h), the machines with their tables (entry, window block, handoff, block store, durability; `util::Lifecycle`), and transaction acceptance waiting for blocks that are still with a task (`WaitForPendingBlocks`).

Threads and pools beside master's: the connect pool (rows 10 to 12, 14, 15, and the prevout fetch), the effects thread, the connector, `coinsahead`, which makes the next node region ahead (4.3), and the four block I/O threads (4.14). The flush helpers are pool tasks. Queues: the pool's task queue, the executor's queue and its backlog wait, the receive task set, the validation-interface queue as on master. Locks: one per shard with a retry before blocking, a mutex per window slot, the script-cache lock, the remembered-marker mutex, the block-index commit guard, the executor's mutex, the block-task mutex, the depot's lock, the bins and the block cache with their own; added since: the connector's mutex, the window's (`WindowSync`), the region-ahead mutex, the flush helpers' and the published tip's. Job 2 counted eleven mutexes and four condition variables at its tip; not recounted.

What the table shows. The store (rows 9 and 10) is 4610 of 11288 lines, with window and flush (9 to 13) 5913; the architecture and crash-safety base (1 to 4, 8) is 2601 and carries no measured gain of its own; everything after the window (14 to 20) is 903. Memory outside `-dbcache` sits in rows 6, 10, 11, 14, 15, 16 with 19, 18, and row 3 up to 64 MiB; in the current cut also the pinned blocks (a ledger account), the region made ahead and the pending-txid table (2^19 places of eight bytes, 4 MiB). Rows 15, 16 and 18 (217 lines) answer one cause, memory made on one thread and freed on another. No row has a ratio from a real sync against master: real figures are the branch against its own earlier tip or a sync with no master run beside it; every ratio against master is from the harness.

### 4.2 The store: entries, records, visibility, locks

No entry is visited a second time when its block is decided (the sweep that did so was the largest worker phase, 36 to 42% of worker wall time, and was removed), with two exceptions that `ConfirmBlock()` walks grouped by shard: the unwritten markers of 4.6 and spends under which an erase is owed. An entry tells whose it is by height: `block_create` on an unspent coin at the coin's height, `block_spend` on a spent entry at the block's height, and the store's `m_confirmed_height`; `Pending()` is "flagged and above the confirmed height". A create is a dirty entry on the list of its height from the apply on (under an owed erase it keeps that change's height). A spend moves the coin into the block's record (`WindowBlock::record`, a vector under the slot mutex) and leaves a dirty spent marker, or erases a fresh entry (but see the spent mark, 4.6). `See(entry, viewer_height)` returns NOTHING, COIN or RECORD; a reader, or an applier below the spender, takes the coin from the record of the slot at the marker's height. `ConfirmBlock()` stores the confirmed height and drops the record; `UnwindBlock()` replays the record backwards.

Lock order: shard, then slot mutex; never two slot mutexes except the lower block of the two taking the higher one's; never slot mutex, then shard. `Record()` cannot leave the shard hold: an item has to be in the block's record before the shard is released, because `RecordCoin()` lookups, displacement in `SpendLocked()` and `CollectRecords()` rely on it. Seen and not changed: `ApplyBatch()` reserves the record to the exact new size on every call, so a continuation's second call reallocates the whole record under the slot mutex.

`GetDirtyCount()` is the shards' dirty total less the `dirty_delta` of the applied slots; `SanityCheck()` asserts it against the entries and the records. `CoinsStore::AccessCoin()` returns the calling thread's copy, good until the thread's next call, never a reference into a shard (an applier can move or erase the coin under the shard's lock alone, whatever lock the caller holds).

Form in the current cut. An entry's state is a set of flags (DIRTY, FRESH, COLLECTED, BLOCK_CREATE, BLOCK_SPEND, OWED, UNWRITTEN, and SPENT) that changes only through the table of `coins_entry_machine.h` (`Transit()`, `Enter()`), and what a viewer sees is the `Reading` that `See()` returns. The coin is stored compact (`StoredCoin`, 48 bytes with 35 script bytes inline; a longer script is on the heap), a map cell is 104 bytes, and nodes are numbered by their 32 MiB region and slot in it. A window block announces the transactions it creates before it applies (`AnnounceCreates`, the pending-txid table), which lets a higher block defer an input made by a block still open without a lookup (`CreatesPendingBelow`, `made_by_pending_block`). A block connected alone reads through `PeekCoinFor` and takes coins its apply misses from its undo data. The clean list gives an entry a second chance before `EvictClean` takes it. Since 22 November (5.2b) a block's record is two lists: a create that went over no confirmed coin is its 36-byte outpoint in `WindowBlock::plain_creates`, and every other change is an `Undo` item of 92 bytes that holds the coin as plain bytes (`StoredCoinData`) and has no destructor, so `ConfirmBlock()` frees the record without reading it; the owner of a script over 35 bytes is kept in `WindowBlock::long_coins`. `UnwindBlock()` takes the record back first and the plain creates after it, each last first. `BlockApplyView::ApplyChanges()` applies from the view's own entries (`CoinsStore::Change` is two pointers), not from a copy of them.

Shards. 256 (`CoinsStore::DEFAULT_SHARDS`, measured in 4.9), picked by key range (4.8). The shard mutex is a plain `Mutex` since 22 November (first as commit 34, then folded into the store commit 11; before it a `ShardLock`, which on a failed attempt yielded and retried for 20 microseconds before it blocked; the removal measured equal at 16 and at 32 workers, 5.2). `ApplyBatch` and `FetchFor` visit shards through `VisitShards` (outpoints sorted by shard, a first pass over the shards free at that moment, a blocking pass for the rest). Byte, entry and dirty totals are in per-thread stripes (`Totals`), so the publisher's size queries read no shard. Harness, 8 workers, when built: 468 blocks/s against 401, 1.17x; time asleep inside tasks 15% to 5%. Under a reindex the publisher is the thread `b-initload`.

Dirty lists per shard are a vector of spans indexed by `height >> 10`; entries keep their exact height and the bound is checked inside the span that holds it (unit case `bounds_are_exact_inside_and_across_dirty_spans`). The tree per shard keyed by height had cost a node per dirty coin; memory per output on the maintainer's run fell from 207 to 147 B.

Judged by reading, nothing measured:

- An entry reached over a list is erased by its key (one probe of the shard's own table).
- The copy of a run's coins into its chunk stays: serialising straight from the entry would hold the shard through the serialisation. A `Coin`'s script holds up to 36 bytes inline (the stored entry 35), so the copy is about a hundred bytes and no allocation for every common output type.
- Scripts above the assumevalid point: a windowed block runs its scripts inline on its one worker, so with `-assumevalid=0` the whole chain runs on the window's threads where master uses `-par`. Never measured as a comparison (the one such run is in 7.1).
- The kernel library does not link `common/system.cpp`; `CoinsStore::Flush` reads `std::thread::hardware_concurrency()` itself; the window no longer does.

### 4.3 Memory made on one thread and freed on another: kept-node lists, the depot, block bins

Kept-node lists. A map node freed by a thread goes on that thread's own list, up to a bound of 1024 (`MAX_KEPT`), and serves its next insertion into whatever shard; nothing is tied to a shard, the accounting per entry stays exact, and the unaccounted part is at most two lists a thread (104 KiB each at the current cell size). The alternative of a pool per shard (upstream's `PoolResource`) was dropped because it strands memory: a pool's free nodes serve only their own shard and the shards are uneven; the cache accounting does not see the stranded part, and accounting by chunks would break eviction (trial figures in 7.3).

The depot. A thread keeps the list it fills and one full list beside it; with both full it gives one to a static depot, with both empty it takes one: a lock for every 1024 nodes. In the current cut the depot has no bound of its own and is where nodes come from at all: it carves new lists from regions of 32 MiB (`NODE_REGION_BYTES`, asked for with the huge page hint, never returned), and once the store is large the next region is made and touched ahead by the `coinsahead` thread. The first cut's depot held at most 64 lists (8 MiB) and read its count without the lock first. Unit case `freed_nodes_serve_another_thread`. Why the kept list overran (likely, not counted): batches are lopsided, a continuation batch mostly spends, a connect-task batch mostly creates. Harness (chain D): 16 workers 502 to 528 blocks/s, parks per run 7,060 to 5,750, resident 440 to 447 MiB; 2 workers 82 to 88. The harness has a few hundred parks a second per worker against ten thousand on the maintainer's machine, so it shows no harm, not the gain (the gain is in 7.4). Talked through and not taken: a freeing thread, another allocator. A region made ahead, not taken in the first cut, is built in the current one (above).

Block bins. A block built on the connect pool is freed by the thread that built it (`BlockBins`, a bin per thread owned by the `ChainstateManager`, emptied before the thread builds its next block and drained by `ActivateBestChain()` once out of initial block download; a node that connects nothing further keeps at most a window of blocks until shutdown). With it, a window worker hashes the transactions of the block it reads itself. Tried beside them and dropped: notifying the connector only for the lowest block in flight (nothing measurable); one bin shared by all threads (0.8x, the thread is the point). Harness figures and lessons in 7.3.

Freed off the path in the current cut besides the bins: the previous block's transactions at the tip go to the pool (`NewBlock(on_pool)`, the commit "net processing: the previous block's transactions are freed off a new block's path"), and block files no open block needs are released by `ReleaseBlockFilesBelow`.

### 4.4 The lagging database: marker, runs, eviction, the owed erase

Three correctness flaws of the first pass, fixed, each with a test:

1. The marker on disk was one run behind the written head in memory, while the lag ceiling and the prune gate decided by the value in memory; a pruned node at the ceiling could crash needing blocks already unlinked. Fix: a run's last write rewrites the marker to (new head, settled head).
2. A coin created and spent inside the lag is erased without a trace; if a reorg disconnects the spend and restores the coin, and the next multi-batch run stops after its first batch, the replay rolls forward along the new chain and the coin is lost. Master cannot do this, since it elides only coins created after the last flushed head. Fix in the current cut: `DisconnectTip()` collects the coins the disconnected block restores that were created at or below the written head, when the block is above the written head on its branch, and hands them to the store (`KeepWritten`). The next run writes them before anything else, alone and without a marker, and a forced flush writes them before it flushes; until then the marker on disk is the old one, whose replay rolls forward along the old branch and leaves the reorganisation to be made again from the undo data. The first cut's fix, a forced full write before the first disconnect (one write of the dirty tier per reorg under `cs_main`), is gone. `feature_dbcrash.py` passed on the current cut (the maintainer's run, 2026-11-21).
3. `ApplySpend` reads the base with the shard unlocked; a lower block's spend applied, written and settled in between would let a higher block spend a coin that no longer exists. Fix: a settle counter per shard, compared across the unlocked read.

A final run that overlaps publication is wrong as such: it would declare the database consistent at a block while a coin its successor spent was never written. A run therefore blocks the publisher (`StartWriteOutRun`: nothing is confirmed into the tier meanwhile; the window's periodic flush between blocks defers the wait and takes it with `cs_main` released, `m_defer_write_out_wait`; every other caller waits where it stands), so starting runs earlier only moves the wait. Overlapping them was judged by reading: the simple rule (cap the settled height below any uncollected dirty entry changed during the run) would hold the written height almost still, since published blocks spend coins of every age.

Runs:

- The write-out target is taken from the total the trigger measures; an event that only evicted does not move the periodic timer.
- The target is one half of that total since 17 November (three quarters before, with no rationale on record). Measured on the maintainer's machine, a reindex to 400,000 at `-dbcache=1024`: against one half, three quarters 0.96x (one pair), a quarter 0.92x, a full write 0.87x; a full write writes 42% more changes than three quarters. The connector is stopped 23% of the span at one half and at three quarters alike. Figures in /work/parallel-validation/performance-suggestions.md.
- Dirty lists per shard as spans of heights (4.2), a clean list with a second chance, the collected mark, and the streamed run (no copy of the run, no second lookup). Invariant: at most one collected chunk outstanding in all, and a collected entry is never erased before its chunk settles.
- A streamed chunk fills across shards to `-dbbatchsize` by an estimate of each change's batch bytes (45 plus the script for a coin, 38 for an erase), so a chunk is one batch, sorted like a flush batch (4.8). Basis: upstream's #31645 (batch sizes from 4 to 256 MiB measured; 32 MiB the default; flushing 5 to 30% faster by storage; nothing gained past 256 MiB); no measurement of my own. A chunk of one shard had never reached `-dbbatchsize`, so the simulated crash of `-dbcrashratio` never fired and `feature_dbcrash.py` failed on its own check that nodes had crashed: the crash hook needs a full batch. `-dbcrashratio` also fires after a non-final call's last batch when that batch is over half the batch size.
- A run collects the shards (`CollectChunk()`, which skips pending entries unless an erase is owed under them) and then the records of the applied slots (`CollectRecords(bound_height, final, ...)`): a confirmed dirty coin an open block spent is written from the record as the coin it still is, an owed erase as the erase. `BeginWriteOut()` scans the records before it concludes there is nothing to write. The final flush runs `CollectRecords(head, final)` and one more `WriteRun` after the flush workers and before the closing marker.
- An erase owed under an open block's create (the block re-creates an outpoint whose spend the database does not have yet) is written by a run as the confirmed value (`OwedValue()`), which is the erase; nothing is held back under an open block.
- After a disconnect the written head is not an ancestor of the head; a run started in that state is forced final, and after a disconnect below the written head every run is final until one has begun (4.11, D2). A prune flush gets a head-only run that moves the written head past the last block that may be pruned.
- Accepted gaps of `CollectRecords()`: (a) a slot decided between its two passes leaves its item for the next run; no block is confirmed during a run in the window, so this needs a caller outside the window. (b) A displaced item whose entry was fresh and erased is skipped; only a failing chain gets there. (c) A coin collected and then spent by an open block before the settle is written twice.
- In the current cut a run is one effect on the executor with its completion under `cs_main`: the restored coins of a reorg, the block-file sync, the block index batch (with the syncs it owes) and the stream. Failures latch (`FlushFailed`, `SyncFailed`, `m_undo_write_failed`, `m_index_batch_lost`) and a later run or flush is refused instead of writing a marker over missing data. `-test=write_out_every_block` makes a run after every block, for tests.

Found while building: the existing unit test `processnewblock_signals_ordering` gives one block object to ten threads; with a block's own checks outside `cs_main` the memo flags in `CBlock` raced under ThreadSanitizer. The three flags are atomic now (`BlockCheckedFlag`).

### 4.5 The window

Retry rule. A worker reads the store while the blocks above it change it and does not look into records, so a valid block below an invalid one can fail in its place. A window block's failure is therefore no verdict: `PublishConnectTip()` sets `m_window_retry_serial`, the window drains, the block is unwound and then connected alone, which decides. A node error is not retried. Cost: an invalid block in the window is validated twice.

No further conditions on when the window opens are to be proposed (the maintainer, on removing a rule that opened it below the BIP34 height: "I don't like that we are introducing more exceptions."). The height clause itself later went for a different reason (4.6).

Undo. A block's undo data is settled on the block's worker once it is complete (`ConnectBlockResult::undo_size`, `SettleUndo`; after the continuation for a block that deferred); harness full reindex 1.24x when built.

Size. The width when `-connectwindow` is 0 is twice the connect workers (`ConnectWindow()`), at most `MAX_CONNECT_WINDOW` 64: the store's window table has a fixed `MAX_WINDOW_SLOTS = 128` that `SlotAt` and `BeginBlock` scan linearly (slot 0 is `NO_BLOCK`; the first cut's `ClaimSlot` is gone), so a wider window is a change to the store, not a constant. Above 32 threads the window is less than twice the pool; above 64 the further threads only take received blocks and the tip's prevout fetch. The byte cap, the byte ledger's Window account in the current cut, is `max(coins cache / WINDOW_BYTES_SHARE (8), MIN_WINDOW_BYTES (32 MiB))`; the eighth is a choice, not a measurement, the floor is about six late mainnet blocks. `WindowBlockBytes(block, num_inputs, block_bytes)` is an estimate, (transactions + inputs) * 512 (`WINDOW_BYTES_PER_ITEM`) but at least the block's own size, plus the view's own usage, about 5.4 MB for a late block. The size floor (15 November, the first memory claim, option A on the maintainer's word) closes the understatement of a block of few inputs with large witnesses; the size comes from where it is known already, the block cache's measure at insertion (`BlockCache::Take()`'s `bytes`) or the length `ReadBlock()` read (`bytes_read`), so the worker reads no block memory for it. The window's cap reads the configured coins cache size, never its usage, so a full `-dbcache` does not narrow the window. The cap is the intended memory bound for small machines. On the harness at `-dbcache=450` it is 56 MiB and held about ten blocks in flight whatever the worker count (a false plateau past ten workers, from Benchcoin's setting); at the default of 1 GiB it is 128 MiB, about 23 of these blocks, and touches only windows of 24 and wider: reindex 164 / 294 / 416 / 492 blocks/s at 4 / 8 / 12 / 16 workers. With `-dbcache=4000` the window fills and a block takes longer from staging to publishable (53 to 64 ms against about 30) with more transactions deferred.

The continuation's fetch. `BlockApplyView::ApplyChanges` clears `cacheCoins` after the first pass, so in `RunContinuation` every input of a deferred transaction is fetched singly (`CheckTxInputs`, `AccessCoin`, `FetchCoin`, `ReadBase`, which calls `CoinsStore::LookupFor` for a window block and `PeekCoinFor` for a block connected alone): a shard lock per coin and on a miss a database Get. In core an input the batched fetch did not return is asked for again singly (`HaveCoin`), a shard lock and a database Get; with the BIP30 check off the database reads left equal the deferred transactions, one missing read each. A trial that keeps the first pass's coins is parked (7.4).

Exceptions on the pool, on the maintainer's word ("if it is not a std::runtime_error inherited exception, then just terminate"): every pool task runs through `util::RunWorkerTask` (util/worker_task.h), which is noexcept and catches std::runtime_error alone; in the first cut `RunConnectTask`, `RunContinuation` and the hashing pool's `FinishChunk` each did so themselves. Consequence: the store's logic_error "Attempted to overwrite an unspent coin" on a pool worker terminates the process where it asked the node to stop before. The std::logic_error handlers and the applied guard are gone. The defect as found is in 8.3.

Bench lines. The current cut logs one `Connect window: ...` line at shutdown and the connector's lines; the first cut's `Worker phases wall/cpu` lines, the lock-wait accounting, the `Block life` line and the exact size count per window block are gone.

Form in the current cut. A staged block's phase is a type (`connect_phase::Staged`, `ReadFailed`, `Deferred`, `Validated`) and its place in the window a state of `node::WindowBlockState` that changes through `WINDOW_BLOCK_TABLE` (node/connect_machine.h). The connect step is planned and finished in separate functions (`PlanConnectStep`, `FinishConnectStep` around `ConnectStep`), and the window rule is one expression: not a retry, the mode is download, the main-chain reorg lock is not held, and the window is wider than one. A block connected alone takes the same step with the lone-block reads of 4.2. The connector waits between blocks, without `cs_main`, on the undo-backlog and pinned-blocks accounts as it does on the window's. `static_assert(MAX_CONNECT_WINDOW < COINBASE_MATURITY)` stands beside the window's constants.

### 4.6 BIP30 in the window, the spent mark, the stretch below BIP34

The gap, as found in 8.3: a window block's BIP30 check did not see a never-written coin that a higher open block had spent.

Closed with a spent mark, on "Ok, implement it." `CoinsStore::BeginBlock()` and `BlockApplyView::BeginBlock()` take `mark_fresh_spends`: an open block's spend of a never-written coin leaves the same spent marker as any other spend instead of erasing the entry. Every other path already understood the marker: `See()` sends a lower viewer to the record (its BIP30 check finds the coin, the block fails and is connected alone by the retry rule), `UnwindItem()` restores, `CollectRecords()` writes the coin from the record. In `CreateLocked()` a pending spent entry under a create returns the conflict before the "overwrite" throw. A block that creates and spends a coin itself leaves the marker too. Validation gives the flag, for a block of the window only, as `OpenBlocksCheckBIP30()`: BIP30 checked for the block or its ancestor `MAX_WINDOW_SLOTS` below. On mainnet the flag is on below BIP34, for the 128 heights above it, and from 1,983,702; on chains whose BIP34 hash never matches (regtest, signet, testnet4) always, so regtest connect figures across this change need the check off: `-test=bip30_off` (`CChainParams::Bip30Off()`) in the current cut, a probe binary in the first (6.3). The view tells the store that its block's creates must not exist yet with `SetCreatesMustBeNew()` (`Change::must_be_new`). Test `fresh_spend_marked_for_lower_create`. No test drives a duplicate outpoint through the real window end to end.

The marker of a never-written coin is dropped at write-out, not erased (`Entry::unwritten`, commit 30): as first built the mark cost 0.85 us a spend at the shutdown write (erases of keys the database never had). And it is freed when its block is confirmed, on "Ok, execute on it.": `ConfirmBlock()`, for a block that marked, walks the record it is about to free, grouped by shard in the current cut, and erases the entry of each spend of a fresh coin if it is still that block's unwritten marker; the `CollectChunk()` and `WipeShard()` branches stay for a run that meets a marker in between. Before that fix the markers stayed in the map until a run or the flush (the 18:00.87 run, 7.1).

With the mark in place the height clause of the window rule was deleted (commit 40, on "Yes, then implement all four."), so the window opens from the start; the stretch below BIP34 had been about 72 s of a run on one thread, about 3% of the blocks.

The two overwrite blocks (91,842 over 91,812; 91,880 over 91,722), read in `CreateLocked()`, `UnwindItem()`, `CollectChunk()`, `CollectRecords()`: over a written coin the create goes over it and an unwind shows the database's coin again; over a confirmed unwritten coin the erase is owed and an unwind leaves the outpoint spent, as upstream's disconnect does; with the first block still open the create is a conflict and the block is connected alone, one window unwind each. An Assume on that path, reachable on the serial path of a build that aborts on Assume, was removed with a comment on the two blocks.

Measured on chain E (6.3) at 8 workers. Small blocks: serial about 950 blocks/s at any worker count, window 2920 / 5890 / 8980 / 7360 at 2 / 4 / 8 / 16 workers (3.1x to 9.4x; the fall from 8 to 16 is in 7.4). Empty blocks: 10500 serial, 17000 in the window (bound by the one publishing thread). With BIP34 at 100000 the stretch runs 8680 to 9250 blocks/s in the window against 930 to 1020 serial; the serial path is at 1190 to 1300 (1240 to 1340 before the mark); shutdown write 156 to 186 ms against 420 ms with the erasing mark. With the marker freed at confirmation: entries at the end 11,573 against 371,573, 8410 to 9860 blocks/s against 7730 to 8580, resident 156 against 199 MiB.

The confirm step split (a probe branch, chain D heavy section, nothing on the series). CPU a block in microseconds at 16 / 4 workers: marker walk 2685 / 3000, record free 217 / 307, everything else about 12. Per item: the record's free 20 to 28 ns, the walk about 0.5 us a marker (a table lookup that misses the cache, an erase, a cell free under the shard's lock). Above BIP34 mainnet marks nothing, so its Confirm is the record's free: 6.2 billion items at 20 ns is 124 s against the 130 s logged. Where blocks are marked (mainnet below 227,931, regtest always) the walk is ten times the free; on mainnet that stretch is 8.8 s of Confirm in all.

### 4.7 The receive side

Goal and rules. The maintainer paused the connect work at the 17:39 run ("17 minutes is good") and set the next subject, the network side of a sync: a local simulation; a generated regtest chain of full-size blocks; served by a bunch of nodes on unmodified master; only the receiving side may change; no signet. The goal: twice master's speed with heavy blocks, master as the client against the branch as the client on the same chain. Stated limit: where master already takes all the links deliver, no receive-side change can double it. A mainnet snapshot route was talked through and not taken (no clean way to fetch one here).

The receive pool. `PeerManagerImpl::HandOffBlock` decides from the 80 header bytes: in initial block download (never with `Options::blocks_on_message_handler`, which unit and fuzz tests set; the first cut keyed this on `deterministic_rng`), with the header's hash in `mapBlocksInFlight` for this peer, the message's bytes go to a task of `m_chainman.m_connect_pool`. Handler and task share `ProcessBlockMessage`: deserialise, mutation check, bookkeeping, the three-hold acceptance, the write, the block cache, the connector's signal, the same functions in the same order as master's handler. The task hashes the transactions itself and leaves the activation to the connector. `RunBlockTask` is `noexcept` and catches only `std::ios_base::failure`. Shutdown: `PeerManager::FinishBlockTasks()` after `connman->Stop()` and before `StopConnector()`. Test: `p2p_ibd_block_handoff.py`.

- Peer rules: a task sets the flag and wakes the handler; the handler checks `m_should_discourage` before the peer's next message and `SendMessages` disconnects first thing; a task drops its block when the peer is gone or marked, and the drop removes the request so the block is asked for again. So at most the pool's width of further blocks from a bad peer are processed, of at most 16 in transit. Bytes that are no block, on the pool, remove the request and disconnect the peer.
- The task limit is `LockTaskLimit()`, the workers minus one, and is a correctness bound, not a tuning value. `Chainstate::DrainWindow` waits for the pool's window tasks with `cs_main` held, and a receive task takes `cs_main`, the only pool task that does; with every worker on a receive task both would wait for ever. A pool of one worker never hands off, which is why the functional suite (`prevoutfetchthreads=1`) sees every block on the handler. The handler waits for a free slot beyond the limit, so memory outside `-dbcache` is at most that many messages and their blocks.
- A block on the pool stays in flight until it is stored; without that the freed handler requested the same blocks again (4130 requests for 2449 blocks). But it does not keep one of the peer's sixteen places: it is flagged (`QueuedBlock::with_pool`) and `SendMessages` counts only the unflagged requests against the sixteen, so the pass that follows the block message asks for the next block. A peer can have sixteen blocks on the way plus the pool's task limit.
- A task always wakes the handler when done (at 24 workers 448 to 507 blocks/s against 371 to 405 without); the stalling timeout is cleared at the hand-off; a second copy of a block whose first is with its task is dropped; the task set is (peer, hash).
- Read and found sound: `NotifyHeaderTip` can deliver two header-tip notifications out of order (cosmetic, already possible against an RPC thread); `FlushStateToDisk(NONE)` in `FinishAcceptBlock` does work only on a pruned node, under `cs_main`, and waits for the effects executor; block-file order shifts by at most the task limit, pruning's bookkeeping is per file; `FinalizeNode` can run beside a task, which holds no connection.
- In the current cut the hand-off is a machine (the handoff states of the port), the mode is a `SyncMode` copy read once per message, transaction acceptance waits for blocks still with a task (`WaitForPendingBlocks`), the stall verdict on a peer is deferred while tasks are outstanding, and `ProcessGetBlockData` keeps a request while the block is being stored.
- The hash pool is gone, since 15 November from the history too (how it left the history: the first-cut review record, 4.11); before that (commit 43): with blocks hashed in their tasks it served only blocks that stay on the handler and the block-file threads of a reindex, and neither it nor the block helper `DeserializeBlock()` showed outside the spread (7.7). `ProcessBlockMessage`, `ReadBlock` and `LoadExternalBlockFile` deserialise with the plain operator over a `SpanReader`, which hashes from the bytes; the loader reads a block's bytes into a buffer first for that. Were the loader ever the bound of a full reindex, its serial rate without the pool is about 0.83x (loader CPU 33 s against 27.5 s). The commit cancels most of commit 13; dropping both in a rewrite of the history is the maintainer's call. Alternatives weighed in the plan and not taken: transactions built on hash threads (strictly immediate disconnect, moves only deserialisation), one lane per peer (nothing for one peer), several message handler threads (outside this branch).

`-test=bip30_off` sets `CChainParams::Bip30Off()`, read by `ConnectBlockChecksBIP30()`, and BIP34 keeps regtest's height 1, so a full `-reindex` accepts the genesis block again (a first form set `BIP34Height = 0`; the genesis block then failed BIP34 on its version and its coinbase, and with no valid block `InvalidChainFound()` aborted on `assert(tip)`). `feature_connect_window.py` reindexes the blocks after the chainstate in both variants.

The kernel's block cache stays a fixed 128 MiB (`DEFAULT_BLOCK_CACHE_BYTES`), not clamped to the coins cache as bitcoind's is, and the C API cannot set it; documented at `ChainstateManagerOpts::block_cache_bytes` and at `btck_chainstate_manager_options_set_database_cache_bytes`. The `-par` help says the window's scripts are not bounded by it.

A requested block that does not deserialise: on the connect pool the request is dropped for the block to be asked for again and the peer is disconnected whatever its permissions, so that it is not asked again to answer the same way; on the message handler master's handling stays, the exception logged and the peer kept until the download timeout. Making them agree would change master's policy for every block message; the reason is in the comment at `RunBlockTask`.

The request rule's test, `p2p_ibd_request_rule.py`, drives a peer's delivery times with mocktime, which both clocks of the rule follow (`NodeClock::now()` at receive, the request time in `SendMessages`): one block until the first arrived, one at a time at four seconds a block, then a count that starts at one, never falls and reaches sixteen within twenty deliveries as it delivers at once, and back to one after a round at four seconds a block. It fails on master (16 asked for at once). Known limit, accepted: with one block outstanding the sample is the round trip, so a peer whose round trip exceeds two seconds stays at one. Also known: the rule limits how many blocks a slow peer holds, not which, so it can still hold the lowest missing block.

Blocks at the tip go to the connector too: `ProcessBlock` activates asynchronously whenever `Options::blocks_on_message_handler` is off, and `SignalConnector(block)` leaves the block just accepted for the connector's next `ActivateBestChain()` as the block in hand, so it is connected from memory and not read back. Test `p2p_tip_connector.py` (the tip's `UpdateTip` on the `connect` thread and a "Using cached block" line; fails when the connector is given no block). Measured with a throwaway harness (`tipbench/tip_latency.py`: a block of 2000 unseen transactions from one test peer, getheaders timed in a loop from another), four rounds each: the longest answer 110 to 117 ms before, 104 to 108 ms after, the tip in 143 to 151 ms either way; an older master binary (4519933391) 110.6 ms with the same 99 ms "Verify 2000 txins". So no cost and no gain: the handler takes the next message, but getheaders, getdata and nearly every other message need `cs_main`, which the connection holds through script verification as on master. A gain at the tip needs the scripts checked before `cs_main` is taken; not attempted.

The per-peer request rule (31 lines in `net_processing.cpp`). In initial block download a peer has outstanding at most one block more than it delivers in two seconds, going by a smoothed time between its block arrivals (weight 1/4; the sample is taken whenever `now >= since`, with a floor of one microsecond), never more than sixteen, and one until its first block arrived. Outside initial block download nothing changes. No message changes; a peer sees fewer hashes in a getdata.

### 4.8 The flush

Why master's shape stalls. The coins database's write buffer is 2 MiB (8 MiB cache / 4, `dbwrapper.cpp`), so every 32 MiB batch is its own memtable and level-0 file; 12 level-0 files stop writes (`kL0_StopWritesTrigger`) until the one background thread has compacted them. With shards picked by salted hash every file spanned the whole key space and each compaction rewrote level 1: one batch in 12.2 stalled. Also `WriteRun` put the marker keys (`DB_BEST_BLOCK` B and `DB_HEAD_BLOCKS` H, which bracket the coin keys C) into every batch, so every batch spanned all coin keys.

What is built:

1. `CCoinsViewDB` remembers the marker it left open (`m_open_marker` under `m_marker_mutex`): the marker is opened once in the first batch and closed by the final one; a `WriteRun` naming the same pair of heads writes coins only; `BatchWrite` and a final run forget it.
2. `ShardIndex` is `(ReadBE32(txid) * shards) >> 32`, the leading four bytes of the txid as the key serialises it, monotonic for any shard count; the in-shard map keeps its salted hash. Side effects: a transaction's outputs share a shard, and the shard pick is grindable (contention on one shard, not complexity).
3. A store batch is whole neighbouring shards and ends between two shards only, at most 32 of them (`MAX_BATCH_SHARDS`, 9.2; the limit keeps the held shard mutexes under ThreadSanitizer's 64); a shard larger than a batch is a run of its own, which `CCoinsViewDB::WriteRun()` cuts at `-dbbatchsize` along the keys. `-dbbatchsize` stays the bound on what the database is given at once; what grows is what a worker holds before it writes, a whole shard's changes (a 256th of the tier), moved out of the map, not a second copy. Each shard's changes are sorted into descending key order (`SortWrites`), walked from the highest shard down, because H sorts above the coins. `SortWrites` orders the outputs of one transaction by the outpoint, which is the key order except past the 16511th output (the key's VARINT of the index grows from two bytes to three and sorts lower bytewise); an overlap there is two pieces touching in one transaction, placed a level higher instead of merged.
4. `MAX_WIPE_WORKERS{4}`: a worker for every two batches of dirty set, capped by cores and shards, each with a contiguous range of shards; the one with the highest range runs on the calling thread, writes the marker's batch, and starts the others once it is in the database; in the current cut the others are tasks of the connect pool, and the caller takes the ranges no worker claimed. A default cache of 450 MiB flushes on one thread; never more workers than cores.
5. The order of a forced flush is the table `FLUSH_TABLE` (`FlushPhase`, node/durability_machine.h). The first cut's second wait for the executor after `SettleWriteOut()` is gone: the final sync of a completed undo file runs inside the undo write's own effect (`FinishUndoFile`).

Why it works: LevelDB places a non-overlapping memtable down to level 2 (`PickLevelForMemTableOutput`) and moves files without rewriting (`IsTrivialMove`); read, not run. A cut in the middle of a shard's walk had defeated it for a shard of more than half a batch (any flush above about 4 GiB at 256 shards): the walk is in the map's hash order and each piece was sorted after the cut, so the two pieces were two batches over the same key range, batches overlapped in a chain down the shards, and the database fell behind and stopped its writers. Hence item 3, not more shards: the maintainer's objection ("Doesn't more shards = more baseline memory, which is not compatible with the low-power setups restriction?") stands unanswered by measurement (only the harness's level peak resident memory from 16 to 4096 shards on a short chain; the per-shard footprint on a long chain, the dirty spans in particular, was never read or measured), and the stalls were a large-cache condition, so raising the default would charge every setup for it.

Upstream: #31875 sorted batches (a 26 GiB dump from 21:56 to 8:27), closed by its author when later benchmarks cast doubt; sipa's objection was the sort's memory at the peak, which sorting a shard at a time does not have.

Unit cases: `flush_batches_are_disjoint_key_ranges`, `flush_never_ends_a_batch_inside_a_shard`; `flush_fills_batches_across_shards` changed minimally (it had counted on the fill inside a shard). Not exercised: a crash in the middle of a flush or between lag runs under the remembered marker; a flush interrupted between the batches of four workers. Runs and probes in 7.1 and 7.2.

### 4.9 Defaults

Measured on the regtest heavy chain (`tune/` harness: `-reindex-chainstate` or a sync from one local peer, pinned with `taskset`; `hardware_concurrency()` does not shrink under `taskset`, so a small machine is emulated by pinning and passing the `-par` it would derive; single runs repeat within 3 to 5%).

| Value | Now | Measured |
|---|---|---|
| Connect pool threads | `GetNumCores()` clamped to [2, `MAX_PREVOUTFETCH_THREADS` 1024] in `node::DefaultPrevoutFetchThreads()` | the rate follows min(workers, cores) on a grid of 1 to 16 cores and workers at dbcache 450; threads beyond the cores add memory (2-core sync resident 128 to 189 MiB from two to eight workers) and no rate. The floor of two: one worker keeps a peer's blocks on the handler, 1.17x slower on one core |
| Window width at `-connectwindow=0` | twice the connect workers (was min(workers, cores)) | reindex 1.14x at two cores, 1.17x at four, 1.2 to 1.3x at sixteen; sync level |
| Window byte floor | 32 MiB under the cache/8 (4.5) | at an 8 MiB cache reindex 88 to 180 blocks/s at two cores, 90 to 585 at sixteen; no change from 256 MiB up. The whole small-cache loss was the budget falling below one block, which narrows the window to a single block; the write-out logic under it cost nothing measurable |
| Shards | 256 (was 1024) | 64 loses 8 to 10%; 256, 1024 and 4096 equal at 8 to 16 workers and under write-out pressure |
| Block cache | the coins cache size clamped to [32 MiB, 128 MiB] when `-blockcache` is unset (`node::DefaultBlockCacheBytes()`, commit 42); 128 MiB from the default `-dbcache` up | at `-dbcache=30` 32 MiB gives 0.81x the resident memory at an equal rate (pruned sync, 8 workers); a default of an eighth of the cache cost 0.96x at `-dbcache=450`, so the rule only shrinks the cache where the coins cache is smaller than it (7.7) |

The maximum of 1024 threads is the maintainer's word (from 32). The kernel's `DEFAULT_PREVOUTFETCH_THREADS` stays 8 for library users, unit tests and fuzz; the functional framework still writes `prevoutfetchthreads=1`. Nothing above 16 cores was measured here; 64 and 1024 threads run correctly on 16 cores with 344 to 436 MiB resident against 218 to 290.

Options the series adds or changes: `-prevoutfetchthreads` (default as in the table, the floor of two being `MIN_DEFAULT_PREVOUTFETCH_THREADS`; 0 for no pool), `-connectwindow` (debug only; 0 for twice the workers, 1 for no window), `-blockcache`, the `-par` help text, and for tests `-test=bip30_off`, `-test=write_out_every_block` and `-test=crash_before_undo_write`.

Corners at the defaults of the day (8 workers then), heavy-section blocks/s, master / branch: reindex at 2 cores 68-78 / 180; at 4 cores 72 / 340; at 16 cores 70 / 600, the same at an 8 MiB and a 450 MiB cache. Sync from one peer: 73 / 140 at 2 cores, 64 / 225 at 4, 61 / 172-185 at 12. Resident memory at harness scale: 181 MiB against master's 150 at sixteen cores, 158 against 121 at two.

What the values do not do:

- Memory outside `-dbcache`: the window's blocks (at least 32 MiB), the block cache (32 to 128 MiB; a fixed 128 MiB in the kernel library), the undo backlog (up to 64 MiB) and the pinned blocks are not in the coins cache budget; each is an account of the byte ledger. The block cache is no larger than the coins cache; one counted byte budget for all parsed blocks was designed and not built, because on the harness memory follows the block cache size and the rate does not (7.7).
- Idle connect workers cost CPU on a sync from one peer: two workers and a window of four gave the same rate as eight and sixteen for 0.75x the CPU. Cause not read.
- Empty blocks at two cores: 12,500 to 13,300 a second against master's 16,500 (0.78x); at sixteen cores 19,000 to 23,000 against 15,200. Where it goes (pass 3 tip, `empty/` harness: 40,000 coinbase-only blocks, `-reindex-chainstate`, `-par=2`, two connect workers, per-thread figures from `/proc` between heights 4000 and 38000):
  - Master is one thread. `initload` spends 59 to 60 us of CPU on a block, of 61 us of wall; the scheduler thread (the validation signals) 19 to 21 us beside it. 80 us in all, 16,200 to 16,600 blocks/s over four runs.
  - The branch spends more CPU on the same block and spreads it: `initload` 30 to 37 us, the two workers 22 to 28 us each, the effects thread 6 us, the scheduler 16 to 19 us; 94 to 118 us in all. Reads, writes and page faults per block equal master's (two reads, one write, the log line), so none of it is I/O.
  - On two cores five threads share what master gives to one and a third: they stand runnable without a core for 91 to 115 us a block, the scheduler thread the longest. Six runs gave 12,600 to 15,700 blocks/s, 0.78x to 0.96x of master, where master's runs differ by 2%.
  - The same binary and settings on four cores: 17,700 (1.09x master), on eight 18,300 (1.12x), with the waiting gone (single runs).
  - Fewer workers do not help: with one worker 12,000 to 12,400, with none 11,300 to 11,800 (0.70x), the same at two and at eight cores. With no worker the branch's own thread spends 81 us on a block where master's spends 59 (1.37x), and the effects thread 8 us more. So the base cost of a block is higher, and the workers hide it only where cores are free.
  - Which code holds the extra 22 to 30 us is not established: no profiler runs on this box. The stage timers (`-debug=bench`, both slowed by the logging, no workers) put about 19 us of it in the connect step up to its end and about 9 us in `Confirm` where master has `Flush`; the stage labels of the branch are taken from timestamps of an earlier stage and say no more than that.

### 4.10 Pruning

Block files: `BlockManager::PinBlockFile()`/`UnpinBlockFile()`, both prune finders skip a file with a write in flight; the prune order of 4.11 (D1, D3).

The prune rule (commit 41). Pruning never passes the coins database's written head, and nothing else limits a pruning node's lag: the fixed ceiling (`LagCeiling()`, 256 in prune mode, a run about every 129 blocks at any cache size) is removed.

- The limit. `FlushStateToDisk` caps the height both finders may prune to at the written head; when the written head is not on the active chain the cap is the fork point with the active chain, so the left branch's blocks above the fork stay too. A file is unlinked only once a block index that records it is on disk (D1 of 4.11), and that index is written after the coins the limit was computed from are on disk, so at no instant does the durable index call a block pruned that a replay from the durable coins database needs.
- Bringing the database forward. When pruning would take files, a write-out run to the prune height is started before the finders choose, and settled before they run. Two conditions gate it. Pruning would take files: a manual request is pending, or `BlockManager::AtPruneTarget()` holds, which repeats the conditions of `FindFilesToPrune()` (the chain above `PruneAfterHeight`, the block files plus one chunk of each kind at the target). And the written head holds a whole file back: `BlockManager::HasBlockFileEndingWithin()` finds a block file ending between the written head and the prune height. The first form, a run at every prune check, fell from 120 to 43 blocks/s; the second, the file condition alone, made a run for each block file from the start of a sync, also while the files were far below the target and nothing could be pruned (found by the review of 4.11 and by the reimplementation, whose gate counts the held-back bytes against the target; the predicate here is the coarser form of the same rule). A node that prunes only on request writes nothing without one. The disk space is checked before the run as before a flush (`CheckDiskSpace`, "Disk space is too low!"), taken from the reimplementation. A run is refused with an error once a flush or a run failed (`FlushFailed()`), where it had reached an `Assert`.
- In the window (current cut): automatic pruning does not wait for the run where it stands (`prune_deferred`); the files are chosen at the next periodic flush (`m_prune_at_periodic_write`). The executor is settled only when files are in fact taken, and a failed run unlinks nothing.
- What it bought (H, 7.7): a pruned sync at `-dbcache=450` at the same rate (121.1 against 120.4 blocks/s) with 0.55x the coins written and 2,269 erases against 232,428; 23 database commits where the ceiling had asked for a flush 1016 times. At `-dbcache=30` the cache drives the runs and nothing changes (37.2 against 38.1).
- The two gaps the ceiling had hidden, both closed: the run's effect committed the block index, with the chosen files already flagged as pruned, before it streamed the coins, so a crash inside the stream left a written head below blocks the index called gone (master has the same window between its index write and its coins flush); and the gate compared heights, not branches, so after a reorganisation deeper than 32 blocks a file holding blocks of the left branch could go while a replay still needed their undo data.
- The benchcoin cost that led here (7.5): `StreamWriteOut` 330 to 395 s of CPU, the database's background thread 1340 s where near-master's has 279 s, 390 GB written for 102 GB received, all with the ceiling. No real-network run without it exists.
- Test: `feature_coins_lag_prune.py`, a crash inside the coins write of a manual prune (the existing `-dbcrashratio=1 -dbbatchsize=300`), then a kill right after files were deleted; each restart rolls forward to the same tip and UTXO hash as an unpruned peer. Never exercised by any test: automatic pruning together with the lag, a written head off the active chain, a crash between the index write and the unlink, a snapshot chainstate.

### 4.11 The review of the series and its fixes

The first cut was reviewed by reading in twelve rounds from 13 to 15 November, four to nine readers each, mostly by area. Every verdict before the fixes was not ACK; the confirmed defects were fixed and folded into the commits that introduced them. The full record of each round (the findings by letter, the fixes, what was left and why, the maintainer's rulings, the backup branches of the time) is `/work/parallel-validation/first-cut-review-record.md`, moved there word for word from this section, from 4.7 and from 5.3 on 2026-11-18; a finding cited by letter elsewhere in this document (D1, D2, V3 and the like) is in that file. The machines port was reviewed again in its own twelve rounds: `/work/parallel-validation/review-plan-full-series.md` with the `full-rN-verified.md` files beside it.

What each round of the first cut changed in the design:

| Round | Date, at | What it changed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 13 Nov, the 43 commits | a create over a visible coin is a conflict and the block is retried alone, which gives master's verdict; a window block where BIP30 is enforced creates with `must_be_new`, and a spend carries the coin it read; failed undo writes and flushes latch and stop every chainstate write; pruning settles the run in flight, unlinks a file only once an index that records it is on disk, and syncs block and undo data first (4.10); `m_fell_below_written` (4.4) |
| 2 (pass 3) | 14 Nov, 4f3d627536 | a failed run ends all writes (`AbandonWriteOut()`); `invalidateblock` settles undo writes and their completions first; outside initial block download `PublishConnectTip()` waits for the effects executor; no prune-gate run for each block file (4.10) |
| 3 | 14 Nov, 3e2a414940 | a deferred transaction over an unspent coin of its own txid fails the block, which is retried alone (a crafted block reached `AddCoin`'s logic_error); a restored coin keeps its creation height (`TagFor()`) |
| 4 | 14 Nov, daaa0d893b | the repeat guard for every non-coinbase transaction of a block that may defer; `CheckBlock()` before the header, as on master; a reorganisation announces the re-added transactions before the connected blocks; a second copy of a block keeps the first sender's record; then a disconnect writes the restored coins the database never held, in place of a forced write before every reorganisation |
