| 5 | 14 Nov, 0678d96583 | that write only when the written head is an ancestor, and carried by the next run (`KeepWritten()`); the coins database's cache no longer resized (5.6); no write-out check inside a reorganisation step; the tip's notification hold one with its publication; `-test=bip30_off` and the window test over it; then the snapshot's written head set at activation |
| 6 | 14 Nov, 35adc2d84b | the uncache tracepoint; one `CheckBlockTransaction()` for both paths |
| 7 | 15 Nov, 897bba5a91 | the download window measured from the connected tip; an owed erase keeps its height (`Retag()`); a lost index batch stops later chainstate writes; the fuzz targets found a use-after-free in `~ChainstateManager` and an unseeded target; then the per-peer pending pong (5.7), `NewPoWValidBlock` before the write with the block served from memory meanwhile, `utxocache:flush` when a run lands |
| 8 | 15 Nov, ca6273c070 | the announced block served only while being stored and not failed; undo writes in flight count for the prune height and `dumptxoutset`; `bip30_off` in `CChainParams`; out of initial block download a tip block is neither measured nor cached |
| 9 | 15 Nov, 4c022ec043 | the prune rule folded into the lag commit; a forced sync after a failed run writes nothing; a queued undo write counts as present (`BlockManager::HasStatus()`); no activation on the message handler for getblocks or getdata in initial block download |
| 10 | 15 Nov, 4ab2fc2e3b | only a coinbase may overwrite a visible coin, and the overwritten coin is kept for the unwind; transaction acceptance waits for the blocks handed to the connector (`WaitForPendingBlocks()`); a run's start no longer settles undo writes under `cs_main` |
| 11 | 15 Nov | that wait on the message handler only (it deadlocked a wallet node); a getdata for a block being stored waits at the front of the peer's queue; an older undo file trimmed and synced in its own effect |
| 12 | 15 Nov, bec2c99f77 | the undo file sync queued at the block-file switch; the kept coin written under an open coinbase overwrite (`OwedValue()`); RPC submissions in initial block download settle the effects queue first; an nTx floor for staged blocks |

What the rounds accepted rather than changed is in 5.7, the test gaps they left in 5.5. The two crafted-block cases of the third round are also on `feature-block-repeated-tx` (2ad2dcfdbe, 5effbafb10 on master) as `feature_block.py` cases, for upstream.

### 4.12 Built and taken off again: the spend hints and the young table

Neither is on the series (tip then 74e8e1db9a, 22 commits). Both were built on 2026-11-18 as commits on top, and both are kept on branches of their own.

**Window matching** (branch `utxo-partial-order-matching-trial`, two commits on 343b027776: 0b53f504d7 and e8c9e32e81; built 2026-11-19 on the maintainer's go after an outside suggestion to match spends inside the window before validating). A lower window block announces its transactions with a source; a higher block's read of an input that lower block has yet to apply takes the coin from the transaction (position kept beside the id in the pending table, checked by the id), checks and applies the transaction in its first pass, and holds back only the spend of the matched coin, which goes with the continuation's apply once every lower block is applied. The spend carries the coin it was checked against (`known_coin`): a coin spent before it or another coin is a store conflict, and the block is connected again alone. `-test=no_window_match` restores the deferral in the same binary. Gates: unit suite 397/397, the window functional tests (matching exercised, 5 to 11 matched spends a run, a crafted invalid block retried alone as before). Harness, `-reindex-chainstate` of chain DX, `-dbcache=1024`, five alternating rounds: deferred transactions 671,000 to 27,000, 713,000 matched spends, no conflict; first form 312.3 against 320.8 blocks/s at 8 cores and 481.6 against 505.5 at 14; after the position table and the merged apply 309.2 against 319.6 (8 cores, -3.3%) and 489.3 against 500.2 (14 cores, -2.2%, ranges overlapping), CPU per run within about half a second. Why it does not pay here (a throwaway probe of the workers' phase CPU): finishing all deferred transactions cost only 1.9 s of about 48 s, since the deferral already skips the wasted lookup (commit 16) and continuations run in parallel; matching moves that work into the first pass and adds a little to the applies. On the maintainer's machine (19 November, -reindex-chainstate to 975,073, -dbcache=30000): 12:08.21 and 17,838 s user against 11:57.62 and 17,354 s on the series tip, with 79,348,729 transactions still deferred (375,315,841 without matching) and 429,680,922 matched spends, 1 conflict, 1 block retried alone: 1.5% slower, 2.8% more CPU. The remaining deferrals are probably inputs whose place in the fixed table of announced ids (2^19 places, a 64-block window) a later id took; not measured. Matching does not pay on mainnet either. Not on the series. `-test=no_window_match` is refused outside regtest; a debug option `-windowmatch` was begun on the branch, uncommitted, and stopped on the maintainer's word.

**The spend hints** (branch `utxo-partial-order-spend-hints`, commits 6c73dd423e and b1d6cc0fd5). The maintainer's assessment of 2026-11-18: fairly useful for IBD; no figure is recorded, and they stay off the series until asked for. They are for a sync whose cache is smaller than the coin set. Without them a write-out run made to free memory writes coins that a block already received is about to spend, and each such coin costs a write and, a few blocks on, an erase: on chain M at `-dbcache=30`, 3.9 of 8.8 million written coins are erased again later.

- The note. `ProcessBlockMessage` notes the inputs of a requested block whose parent is known in `ChainstateManager::m_spend_hints` (`SpendHints`): a direct-mapped table of 2^20 atomic words, 8 MiB, allocated by the first run for memory and not counted as cache. An entry is void once the chain is at the spender's height; the horizon is 1024 blocks. Until the first run for memory a note is one atomic load.
- The run. A run started for memory pressure, and not waited for by pruning, passes `CoinsStore::Hold` with an allowance of half the bytes it was to free. It leaves a confirmed coin that is fresh, created above the height the database held before the run and within `HOLD_DEPTH` (144) of the run's head, if a note names it or an open block spent it. The coin stays dirty, so it is not evicted and counts in the budget. Periodic, final, forced and prune runs persist everything as before, and no window rule changed.
- Crash safety. The run settles one block below the oldest coin it left, so the existing replay covers a crash. Because a holding run settles below changes it wrote, a disconnect below the highest bound written forces a final run (`m_written_through`), and so does a coin restored during a holding run in the range where the settled height is not known yet (`TagFor`).
- Where they act. Only where blocks arrive from peers and the cache is under pressure. A node whose cache holds the whole set never makes a run for memory, and `-reindex-chainstate` receives no block from a peer, so neither shows them (the full-memory reindex run of 7.10 is the same with and without).
- Measured 19 November, ported onto the series tip (branch `utxo-partial-order-spend-hints-trial`, 8e7bf94f87 and ded5544e01 on 343b027776, cherry-picked without conflict; unit suite 397/397, the pruning and window functional tests pass). Harness SYNCS of chain DX (three master peers, client on 8 cores), three alternating rounds against the tip, `cpu/hints_ab.sh`, log `cpu/hints-ab-2026-11-19.log`. At `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5`: 45.4 against 41.2 blocks/s (medians; 44.0 / 46.0 / 45.4 against 41.2 / 41.1 / 41.7, every hints round faster than every tip round), client CPU 177 to 181 against 195 to 198 s, coin changes written in runs 13.3 against 16.85 million (-21%), 1,517 against 1,937 MiB, erases 6.57 against 8.34 million, peak RSS 434 to 441 against 476 to 495 MiB. At `-dbcache=450`: 231.4 against 236.6, no write-out run in either (chain DX's coin set fits the cache), so the hints have nothing to act on; a test there needs a chain whose coin set exceeds the cache, or a real sync. A 10% gain at the harness floor, with fewer writes and less memory; whether it carries to 450 and mainnet is open. Local mainnet syncs the same day (`ibd/local_hints.sh`, log `ibd/local-hints-2026-11-19.log`): a plain master serving node with mainnet to height 250,000 (11 GB of blocks, downloaded once; `-whitelist=download@127.0.0.1`, no outbound connections), the client on 8 cores from a fresh datadir with `-dbcache=100 -maxmempool=5 -minimumchainwork=0 -assumevalid=<block 250,000>`, three alternating rounds: wall 2:35.50 / 2:27.71 / 2:27.75 for the tip against 2:29.14 / 2:30.79 / 2:30.68 for the hints, 854.7 blocks/s over heights 150,000 to 250,000 for both (one hints run 847.5), 45 write-out runs and 14.02 million coin changes written in every run of both. The hints held nothing ("0.0 MiB left for blocks received to spend" in every run): each run's bound was 3,800 to 9,600 blocks below the head when it started, so every coin it wrote was far older than the 144 blocks (`HOLD_DEPTH`) a held coin may be, the bound that keeps the written head, and with it a crash's replay, close behind. On chain DX at 30 MiB the cache holds only a few dozen heavy blocks of changes, so the coins a run writes are young. What decides is how many blocks of changes the cache holds: with today's blocks (thousands of coin changes each) a 1 to 4 GB cache holds on the order of a thousand blocks or more, so the hints as built would rarely act at typical sizes, and help only caches holding under about 144 blocks of changes (an estimate from the mechanism, not measured). A deeper `HOLD_DEPTH` would reach further only by letting the written head fall further behind (longer replays), a change to crash recovery. Dropped on the maintainer's word (19 November, "lets revert this change again"): they help only caches holding under about 144 blocks of changes, and a deeper limit would cost crash replay length, more reorgs forcing a full write and more memory pressure for an unmeasured gain. They were never on the series; both branches stay as a record (`utxo-partial-order-spend-hints`, `utxo-partial-order-spend-hints-trial`).

**The young table** (branch `utxo-partial-order-young-table-trial`, commits 08c05b4dca and 7abcf2575f). A small table for each shard, beside its main one, for the coins blocks create: option 2 of the maintainer's grouped-commit notes. Measured slower by the maintainer (7.10) and dropped (8.1).

- The table. Inside each shard's map, under the shard mutex: an array probed in line with twice as many slots as the entry limit, each slot the hash beside the cell, so a probe for another key reads no cell. It is made with the first entry and freed by a flush. The slot of a hash is its product with a constant, because the hashes of one transaction's outputs follow each other and would form one run. The cells are those of the main table and never move.
- The path. A block's create that is neither BIP30-checked (`must_be_new`) nor an overwrite is made there without a probe of the main table, as master's `AddCoin(possible_overwrite=false)` takes a create for fresh without reading the database. Every lookup probes the young table first. A full table is moved on to the buckets, which are asked for a stretch of 32 slots ahead; a flush moves it as well. A coin spent before that never touched the main table: not at its creation, not at the spender's lookup, not at its erase.
- The guard. The main table can hold an outpoint that such a create makes only as the spent marker of a disconnected block's output. From the first marker a change without a block identity leaves until a `Flush()` has emptied the store, creates go to the main table as before.
- The limitation. A txid made twice at a height where BIP30 is not checked (excluded by BIP34) would leave two entries for one outpoint; `SanityCheck()` asserts there is none, nothing else looks.
- The switch and the counters. Debug-only `-coinsyoungtable=<n>` entries a shard, default 0 for no table. At 1024 the 256 shards hold about 262 thousand entries in about 8 MiB of slots, at 4096 four times that. Every coins flush logs the totals since the start: made, ended there (never in a main table), moved on, lookups answered there against lookups that went on. On regtest the table fills only with `-test=bip30_off`.

**The compact entry** (on the series since 2026-11-19 as commits 26 baf4dc1de2 and 27 890928840b, on the maintainer's word, messages without the trial note and the figures; built on branch `utxo-partial-order-compact-trial` on 343b027776; the `-dbcache` clamp for the 256 GiB node limit left out by the maintainer; started 2026-11-19 on the maintainer's go, measured on chain DX only, by the maintainer's word). Aim: fewer bytes per coin, so that a cache sized from the machine's memory (RAM less the node's overhead, the chainstate's size on disk for the page cache and a margin, floor 1 GiB, cap the whole set; discussed the same day, not built) holds more of the set. The layout before: about 125 to 130 bytes an entry without a script's heap memory (chain link 8, outpoint 36 padded, Coin 48, write-out height 4, flags 2 padded, list links 16, bucket about 8), plus 48 to 64 for a script over CScript's 28 inline bytes. Three steps planned: (1) scripts inline up to 35 bytes, (2) 32-bit indices for the chain, bucket and list links, (3) the two heights and the flags in one word.

- Step 1, commit 30df57394a: `StoredCoin`, 48 bytes like a Coin, the script inline up to 35 bytes (every standard script, P2WSH and P2TR included), longer ones on the heap with their length; bytes unchanged, lookups hand out a Coin. Round-trip unit test `stored_coin_round_trip`; unit suite and the store and window functional subset pass. On chain DX (`cpu/compact_ab.sh`, log in the job tmp `cpu/compact_ab.log`, 8 cores): 137.4 against 145.5 bytes per coin at height 4,281 (-5.6%; chain DX's coins mostly have short scripts, so mainnet's taproot share would save more, not measurable here); -reindex-chainstate at 1024 319.6 against 321.3 blocks/s (medians of three); sync from three peers at 1024 233.8 to 237.2 against 234.7 to 240.2; at -dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5 42.1 / 42.4 / 42.5 against 41.7 / 42.4 / 41.3 blocks/s, write-out runs 147 / 144 / 148 against 157 / 157 / 156 (about 6% fewer). Neutral in speed, smaller in memory.
- Step 2, commit 5cc5657f49: 32-bit cell numbers for the chain link, the bucket array and the list links (region number high, slot low; node regions aligned to their 32 MiB size with their number in the first slot; a constant-initialized table of 2^13 region addresses, 64 KiB of static memory supplied page by page, 4 KiB per 16 GiB of nodes; abort past 256 GiB of nodes). StoredCoin keeps its amount as bytes so the cell packs to 104 bytes (120 before), bucket 4 bytes (8). Test `node_numbers_span_regions` (700,000 entries, about 73 MiB, three regions or more). On chain DX (`cpu/compact_ab2.sh`, log `cpu/compact_ab2.log` in the job tmp): 112.9 against 145.5 bytes per coin (-22%). With the region table first behind a function-local static, -reindex-chainstate at 1024 was 4.3% slower (315.0 against 329.3) and syncs at 1024 3 to 8% slower; with the constant-initialized table (folded in) 320.3 against 324.2 (step 1 318.4) and syncs 236.8 / 237.0 / 229.8 against 240.9 / 235.1 / 232.6: equal within the noise. At -dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5 (measured before the table fix): 43.5 / 44.2 / 43.3 against 42.8 / 41.7 / 42.5 blocks/s, write-out runs 121 against 155 (-22%), client CPU 178 against 192 s (-7%).
- Step 3 (heights and flags in one word) not built: after step 2 the entry has 2 bytes of padding and the allocator's cells are multiples of 8, so it saves nothing; without step 2 it saves nothing either (the pointers keep the entry at 72 bytes).

### 4.13 Sketch, not built: a large block validated in chunks (20 November)

Asked by the maintainer after the steady-state breakdown (5.2a: a heavy tip block with a full mempool spends about 8.4 ms in the serial core and 25 ms in the connect as a whole). The idea: split a large block's transactions into chunks and validate them as neighbouring entries of the window, so that the core's serial loop is spread over the connect pool. A design sketch only; nothing is built.

What is unavoidable, in any design: transactions spending outputs of earlier transactions in the same block (about a third of outputs are spent in their own block, 7.6); the per-block rules (fees against the coinbase, the sigop limit, undo data in transaction order). Not a problem: a double spend inside a block makes it invalid whichever spend comes first, so detection is enough, with the exact verdict from a serial re-run, as the window's conflicts already work. What is the series' own: the block as the unit of identity (window slot, change record, conflict order by height, AnnounceCreates per block) and a block-shaped core. Ruben's out-of-order design (7.6) has the transaction as its unit and would fit better, but needs a set that keeps spent outputs (against C4) and adds outputs on arrival, which the matching trial (4.12) found no faster here. The sketch therefore extends the series instead.

- Unit. A window entry is a chunk: a block's transactions [begin, end), the coinbase in the first. A block is split when it has more than a threshold of transactions (a property of the data, so C17 holds: a large block in initial block download is split as at the tip), into about as many chunks as the connect pool has workers, at transaction boundaries, each chunk about equal in inputs.
- Identity and order. The store's order key becomes (height, chunk) where it is height now: window slots, change records, the conflict rule (the lower key stands), the creates announced by an entry (AnnounceCreates, CreatesPendingBelow). A block's chunks are confirmed together, by its last chunk's publication; nothing outside the window sees a part of a block.
- Dependencies inside the block. A chunk's input made by a transaction of a lower chunk of the same block is deferred, exactly as an input made by a lower block is now (the lower chunk announced its txids), and finished by the continuation once that chunk is applied. A spend of an output made earlier in the same chunk stays inside the chunk's view, as now.
- The core. ConnectBlock takes a range of the block's transactions and returns the range's partial results: fees, sigop cost, input count, undo entries for its transactions, its deferred transactions. The per-transaction checks are unchanged (amounts, sequence locks, BIP30 per output, scripts inline on the worker as for any window block). The checks of a total move to the block's publication: the sum of the chunks' fees in money range, the sum of their sigop costs against MAX_BLOCK_SIGOPS_COST, the coinbase amount against fees plus subsidy (the core already defers this check when a block has deferred transactions: coinbase_check_deferred).
- Undo data. Each chunk's undo entries are its transactions' slots; the publication of the last chunk concatenates them in transaction order into the block's CBlockUndo before its write is queued (the core already leaves empty slots for deferred transactions and fills them in the continuation).
- Failure. Any chunk that fails, or a conflict between chunks, sends the whole block back to be connected alone (unsplit), which decides, as a window block's failure does now. A block that is invalid fails there with master's verdict and state.
- Signals and the block index. One BlockConnected, one undo write, one UpdateTip per block, at the last chunk; the chunks are invisible outside the connector and the store.

Work, in order: the (height, chunk) key through the store and the window; the range form of ConnectBlock with open totals; the publication's totals and undo assembly; the split rule; tests that a split and an unsplit block give the same state, undo data and verdict (valid, a double spend across chunks, a sigop total over the limit only in sum, a coinbase over fees only in sum).

Expected effect (estimates from the measured shares, not measured):
- Steady state (full mempool, scripts cached): the serial core of a heavy block, 8.4 ms, over about 8 chunks becomes roughly 2 to 3 ms (a fixed cost per extra entry of about 0.3 ms from the empty-block measurement, and a serial tail for transactions spending same-block outputs across chunks, finished by continuations). About 5 ms of about 50 ms receipt to tip: around 10%. The other 40-odd ms (mempool cleanup about 11 ms, the about 25 ms before the connect) are untouched.
- A whole block with cold inputs and scripts to run: the core, 44 ms on a heavy block, holds the coin reads and the input checks serially while the check queue runs the scripts; chunks would run both on 8 workers. Perhaps 20 to 30 ms of about 115 ms, 20 to 25%, the largest gain; depends on the serial tail.
- Initial block download: no gain expected and a small loss possible. The window already keeps the workers busy with whole blocks; chunks add entries, announcements and continuations for every large block. A gain only where the window cannot fill (a single large block holding up the connector, or a window narrowed by its byte limit), which the measurements found rare. A rule that split only when workers are idle would avoid the loss but asks the pool's state, near the edge of C17; to be decided.
- Small blocks (under the threshold): unchanged.

### 4.14 File I/O at the edges

Written on 2026-11-23 against the code at tip b5fc79e546 (commits 9 to 18 of 9.4). The rule itself stands in `validation.h` under "File I/O", after O15, and the code is the authority where the two differ; this section gives the reasons, follows one block through the files, and says which component owns which piece. How the work came about, step by step, is in the head row; the harness figures are in the three bullets under 5.2 ("Block I/O pool", "File work off cs_main", "Block I/O threads 2 against 4").

**The philosophy.** The validation side is a set of state machines whose transitions run under `cs_main`. A transition should only decide and record: it reads the block index, picks a position, flips a status, and is over. Everything that touches a block or undo file (reading, writing, allocating space, trimming, syncing, unlinking) is work that takes as long as the disk takes, so it is made between two states, with `cs_main` free, and the only thing that waits for it is the thing it is made for: the one block being stored, the one window slot being filled, the one reorganisation step being prepared. The maintainer set this as a goal of the series on 22 November ("pushing IO to the edges is something I would very much like to achieve") and confirmed that it is meant strictly. Four choices follow from it:

- Blocking threads, not asynchronous I/O interfaces. The maintainer's words: "A pool of blocking threads seems fine" and "as little OS-specific hacking as possible". A read or a write blocks the thread it is made on, so it is given threads that do nothing else, and the connect workers stay on the processor. There is no io_uring, no AIO, no direct I/O; the one system-specific call in all of it is the page cache advice of step 7 below, and nothing depends on it.
- Two lanes, split by whether order matters. File work that must happen in a fixed order with other things (an undo write before the notification of its block, a sync before the index batch that records the bytes) goes through the ordered effects executor, one thread, `BlockManager::m_effects`. File work that has no order with anything else (a block's bytes read or written, a pruned file removed) goes to the block I/O pool, `BlockManager::RunBlockIO()`, four threads named `blockio` (`BLOCK_IO_THREADS`). A task of the pool never takes `cs_main`, because a thread that holds it may be waiting for another task queued behind this one, and it lets no exception leave it.
- Under the lock, arithmetic; off the lock, the system calls. Claiming a position is a sum and a note of which chunks the position reaches (`ClaimChunks()`); whoever writes there allocates the space first (`Preallocate()`, under a leaf mutex of the file sequence). A full disk is therefore found by the write and no longer by the claim; the fatal error is the same.
- The rule is checked, and its exceptions are named in the code. A rule of this kind erodes silently, so with DEBUG_LOCKORDER the block manager's lock-free file functions stop the node when their thread holds `cs_main`, and every place that does file work under the lock on purpose is a scope that says why (below).

What the rule does not try to do: it does not reorder the block write behind the connect (asked and withdrawn by the maintainer, "No, don't do that": O2 and O11 rest on a block being written before it is recorded as stored), and it does not optimise the writes and reads of a tip block or an RPC (the maintainer: "little point in optimizing those"); those stay on their callers' threads, already outside `cs_main`.

**The lifetime of a block, by its file work.** The path is that of a block received during the initial block download; where another path differs it is said at the step.

1. *Received.* The message handler hands the block's bytes to a receive task on the connect pool (4.7). The task deserialises and hashes, and runs `ChainstateManager::AcceptNewBlock()`: the block's own checks without the lock, a hold of `cs_main` that accepts the header, the contextual checks without the lock again, and a second hold that claims the block's place on disk. The claim is arithmetic. It also pins the file (`PinBlockFile()`), so that pruning leaves a file alone while a write is in flight for it. No file is touched.
2. *Written.* `WriteNewBlock()` runs on a block I/O thread: it allocates the chunks the bytes need and writes them. This is the stretch between "being stored" and stored of the block store machine (O2). No lock is held and nothing waits for it except this block. Outside the initial block download, and for a block the handler keeps, the same three functions run one after another on the caller's thread (`ProcessNewBlock()`), the write again with `cs_main` released.
3. *Stored.* `FinishNewBlock()` runs on a connect pool worker again, queued at the front since the block is nearly done: one hold records the block as stored, unpins the file, puts the block into the block cache and signals the connector. If the write position left a file while a write was still in flight for it, the file's trim is queued when its last write is in (`m_blockfiles_to_trim`, `UnpinBlockFile()`); otherwise it was queued at the file switch (`QueueBlockFileTrim()`). The trim is the executor's and comes without a sync: the file stays marked unsynced until whatever records its blocks in the block index syncs it.
4. *Staged for the window.* The connector stages the block. A block that is in memory (passed in, or in the block cache) needs no file. One that is not, which is every block of a `-reindex-chainstate` and any block the cache let go, has its bytes read on a block I/O thread (`Chainstate::ReadForConnectTask()`, `ReadBlockBytes()`), and the connect task is submitted with the bytes; the worker parses them (`ParseBlock()`). The split of `ReadBlock()` into its I/O half and its parsing half exists for this hand-over. With a pool without workers the connector reads the block itself, and so it does for a block connected alone that is not in memory; in both cases it holds no `cs_main` over the read.
5. *Connected and published.* The workers validate against the store and read no file; the one blocking I/O left to them is a coin database read for an old coin. At publication, under `cs_main`, the block's undo position is claimed (`ClaimUndoPos()`, arithmetic again) and the undo write is queued as an effect. From here on the availability checks count the undo data as present (`HasStatus()`), because the write is in order before anything that may read or prune it.
6. *Undo written.* The executor writes the undo data with no lock held (`WriteBlockUndoAt()`, which allocates like the block write) and its completion records the position under `cs_main` (`OnUndoWritten()`); the block's `BlockConnected` rides the same queue behind it (C6). When this write is the last of its undo file, the same effect trims and syncs the file (`FinishUndoFile()`), ahead of any write-out run queued after it. A block connected alone queues its undo write before its apply (the tip commits, 9.4).
7. *Let go of the page cache.* The thread that read a window block tells the system that the block files sixteen below the one it read (`BLOCK_FILE_RELEASE_LAG`) will not be read again soon (`ReleaseBlockFilesBelow()`). Advice only; where the system has no such call nothing is done.
8. *Made durable.* A write-out run's effect makes the syncs its block index batch owes (`SyncBatchFiles()`) on the executor before it commits the batch, so the index never records bytes a crash could lose (I10, the durability machine). A forced flush syncs under `cs_main`, by a caller that waits for exactly that; it is one of the named exceptions.
9. *Pruned.* The files to prune are chosen and the block index that no longer records them is written under `cs_main` as before (4.10); the files themselves are removed on a block I/O thread afterwards. `pruneblockchain` removes them inline, since its caller waits for the result. A file a write position is in is never pruned (`IsCursorFile()`), so a pruned file's number is never claimed again, which both the late removal and the chunk map rely on.
10. *Disconnected, if a reorganisation reaches it.* A step that will disconnect is planned in one hold (`Chainstate::PlanReorgReads()`): up to `REORG_READ_AHEAD` = 16 blocks with their undo data from the tip down, and up to 16 blocks of the new branch from the fork up to the first that gives the tip more work. They are read on the block I/O threads with no lock held (`ReadForReorg()`), and the next hold checks that tip and target are still the planned ones and takes each block by its index entry. `InvalidateBlock()` reads each block it disconnects before that block's hold. What was not read ahead is read under the hold as on master: the blocks past the first 16 on either side, a read that failed, and a step that follows one whose branch was invalid, where the locks are not released in between.

**Who is responsible for what.**

| Component | Owns | Does not do |
|---|---|---|
| A transition under `cs_main` (acceptance, publication, the flush's decisions) | deciding and recording: positions claimed by arithmetic, pins, the queueing of trims and effects, the block index in memory | open, read, write, allocate, trim, sync or unlink a file |
| Block I/O pool, `RunBlockIO()`, four `blockio` threads | unordered, blocking file work: the write of a block received in the initial block download, the read of a window block that is not in memory, a reorganisation's read-ahead, the removal of automatically pruned files | take `cs_main` or any listed lock; keep an order; parse or validate |
| Effects executor, `m_effects`, one thread | ordered file work and what must follow it: undo writes with their completions and notifications, the trim of a file that is finished, the trim and sync of a finished undo file, write-out runs with the syncs their index batch owes | anything that needs no order; its completions take `cs_main` only by `TRY_LOCK` or on a thread that already holds it |
| Connect pool workers | parsing, hashing, validation; a receive task's accept and finish steps; coin database reads | block or undo file I/O |
| Connector thread | staging: it decides whether a block needs a read and queues it; plans a reorganisation's reads and waits for them between two holds | read files itself, except with a pool without workers |
| Block manager | the mechanism: claim (`ClaimChunks()`), allocation (`Preallocate()`), pins, the deferred trim, `ReadBlockBytes()`/`ParseBlock()`, and the check | policy: it does not decide when or on which thread a caller does its work, only refuses the work under `cs_main` |
| Block cache | keeping a received block in memory between its write and its connection, so that step 4 needs no file on a network sync | bound the window's blocks (4.9) |
| A caller that waits for the result itself | its own file work, on its own thread: tip and RPC block writes, getdata and RPC reads | hold `cs_main` over it, unless it is one of the named exceptions |

**The check and the exceptions.** `AssertFileWorkOffMain()` sits in the seven block manager functions that take no lock themselves: `OpenBlockFile()` and `OpenUndoFile()`, which every read and write passes through, `Preallocate()`, `SyncBatchFiles()`, `FinishUndoFile()`, `UnlinkPrunedFiles()` and `ReleaseBlockFilesBelow()`. With DEBUG_LOCKORDER they stop the node when their thread holds `cs_main`; without it nothing is checked. A caller that works under the lock on purpose opens a scope `BLOCK_FILE_WORK_UNDER_CS_MAIN(why)`, and the scopes are the complete list of the rule's exceptions, twelve in production code: at the start, the opening of every block file, the removal of files pruned before the stop, verifying (also for `verifychain`) and the replay of an interrupted write-out; a block accepted in one call (the external block loader and tests); the block write and the undo write in one call (the genesis block, tests and benchmarks); the undo data of a block disconnected under the lock where it was not read before, the block to disconnect that was not read before the hold, and a reorganisation's block to connect that was not read before the hold; `pruneblockchain`, which waits for its files to be removed; and the block read in `SendMessages` for a compact block announcement of a tip that is not the most recent block in memory. The prose list in `validation.h` names the same set by what the caller is doing. The functions that require `cs_main` by annotation, the syncs of a forced flush, are exceptions by that annotation and carry no check. Seven tests that read or write under a hold of their own carry a one-line marker (head row).

**Is it pure?** Not strictly, and the gaps are the list above. The initial block download path is: accept, write, store, read, undo write, trim, sync and prune each run with `cs_main` free, and the check holds on that path. The reorganisation path is pure for the first 16 blocks on each side and falls back to master's reads under the hold beyond that. The fallback was kept on purpose: it is the correct behaviour for a failed or missing read, it bounds the memory a reorganisation holds, and a deeper reorganisation is rare enough that nothing was built for it. The start-up work and the RPC exceptions hold the lock over file work as master does, with a caller that wants exactly that result.

**Beside #36000** ("validation: prefetch blocks while connecting", l0rinc, open on 2026-11-23; read, not built). It goes in the same direction for the read side only: a `node::BlockFetcher` per `Chainstate` with a pool of its own (`blockread`, two threads by default, `-blockfetchthreads`), which reads the next blocks ahead while one connects and, on a reorganisation, the first blocks of the new branch. It is looser than the rule here: `ConnectTip()` waits for the read under `cs_main`, and a speculative read that failed is retried synchronously under the lock. Our fallback is therefore compatible with it; both keep master's read under the hold as the last resort. If it lands first and the series is rebased on it: its retry needs one `BLOCK_FILE_WORK_UNDER_CS_MAIN` marker, the connect half of the reorganisation read-ahead would duplicate its prefetch and can go while the disconnect half stays (it has nothing for that side), the two pools fold into one, and its `catch (std::exception&)` in the read task is against the rule of 4.5. The conflicts are in `ConnectTip()`, `ActivateBestChainStep()` and `ActivateBestChain()`.

**What is not known.** No measurement on a slow disk exists for any of this; the harness's block files sit in the page cache, so its figures (5.2) show that the moves cost nothing there and say little about what they gain where a read or a sync takes milliseconds. Never exercised by a test: a disk that fills between a claim and its write, a read-ahead that fails, a target that changes between a reorganisation's two holds, and `InvalidateBlock()`'s use of its reads. The full functional suite and `feature_dbcrash.py` last ran before all of the file work (head row).

## 5. What is open

### 5.1 The design review's answer

No different design in view reaches equal or better performance with every constraint intact. The simpler alternatives drop the low-end write-out logic or a constraint; the one idea both simpler and inside the constraints (inputs ordered by shard) would replace the centre of the series. The series' open points are at its edges: memory accounting, threads in question, figures never measured. A second comparison round with the outside designs and a second independent design gave the same answer (7.7). The passes behind this are in 7.6.

Verdicts that need no measurement and are still open (the pool exception defect and the BIP30 gap with the serial stretch below BIP34 were fixed, 4.5 and 4.6; everything not named here or in 5.2 is "keep"):

| Component | Verdict | State |
|---|---|---|
| Blocks in memory: window cap, block cache, receive tasks, bins | one byte budget inside or derived from `-dbcache` | not built; the block cache default follows the coins cache instead (4.9, 7.7). Receive tasks and bins stay uncounted |
| Undo backlog | state a byte bound | it had one: 64 MiB (row 3 of 4.1) |
| Block-index states (row 4) | remove (-177 lines) or keep as a C1 goal | dropped on 21 November (8.4); row corrected 22 November, it had stayed "open" |
| Prune ceiling | remove after one reading of the prune paths | removed (4.10) |

Ran on the harness (7.7), and so left this table: the effects executor's removal (lost, it stays), the hash pool's removal (done), the memory batch and the pruned sync of 7.6, the profile of the low-end corner. The rest did not run.

Not on the series; whether the maintainer's machine (the publisher 23 to 30% late for a core there) differs is not measured | small |

Taken: commit 34, 3a957e1318, same tree as the trial; the branch deleted; folded into commit 11 the same day. Closed | -37, taken |

### 5.2a Tip connect performance

The maintainer's question: why is the tip no faster than master when initial block download is, given the allocator, depot, table and huge-page work? My answer, from memory and not checked against the code: (1) the tip latency harness (about 145 ms against 141.5 on an older master, verification about 99 ms on both) had largely uncached scripts, which run on master's own check queue; (2) the allocator, depot, per-shard tables and huge pages recovered costs the store itself brought (cross-thread frees, contention, misses in a large table), which matter with many workers in initial block download, not for one block on one thread against a warm cache, where master's CCoinsViewCache never had them; (3) the store adds per-block work at the tip: the tip bench (commit 47) has prefetch and core within a few percent of master's shape, Confirm() about 1.8x master's flush (about 1 ms on block 413567), the round trip 1.14x; the hand-offs (connector, effects) measured at no cost. Correction made in the discussion: a real tip block is rebuilt from the mempool and hits the script cache (WarmScripts is only about 1.5x NoScripts), so the UTXO side is a large share of a realistic tip connect, and the series may sit slightly behind master there.

Levers discussed, under C17 (no tip or solo-block introspection; a single-block gain applies in initial block download too, section 2):
- An unwind driven by the block's undo data and its own outputs, guarded per entry by the block's identity, instead of a per-change record built on the apply path; previous flags restored conservatively (dirty, not fresh), an occasional redundant write, as with the owed erase. Moves cost from every block to the rare failing one; helps the window as much as the tip.
- A prefetch sized by its misses: one pass taking each shard's lock once copies the inputs the tier holds (as BlockApplyView::Prefetch()); only the misses go to parallel database fetches, and only when there are enough to pay for the pool's wake-up. Decided by the data, the same for every block.
- Ruled out by C17: a solo-block confirm, and connecting inline when the connector is idle.

First steps when taken up: a split timing of Confirm() (record building, per-shard locking and tagging, the confirm itself) and a warm-scripts variant of the tip bench through the store and through master's overlay, to size what the levers can buy. Nothing started; waits for the maintainer.

**Measured and partly fixed, 19 to 20 November** (on the maintainer's go; plan and rulings in memory `tip-connect-plan-rulings`; tools in `/work/utxo-partial-order-harness/tip/`: `prep.py`, `prep_main.py`, `replay.py`, `rounds.sh`, `rounds_main.sh`, `split.sh`, `timeline.py`, `slim.sh`; logs `rounds-*.log`, `slim-fix3.log` there).

- Correction of 20 November: the bench figures of this item were taken in `build-clang`, which has `-DDEBUG_LOCKORDER` (every lock acquisition through a global mutex), and are void wherever several threads take locks. In the clean build (`build-bench`, bench enabled since): round trip 11.1 against 9.5 to 9.9 ms (1.14x, as the old figure above), core 3.5 ms with the parallel fetch and 3.6 without (no convoy). The node figures below were always taken in the clean build and stand.
- The bench first, in the debug build (void, see the correction above; block 413,567, `ConnectBlockMainnetTipStore` against `...TipOverlay`, phase probe never on the series): the store's tip round trip was 2.7x the overlay's at every commit since the bench exists (the 1.14x above was the old series). Connect path 15.0 against 5.3 ms: core 9.7 against 3.6 (the parallel fetch against the store: the core catches up with the fetch threads and sleeps and wakes per input; with the store's own Prefetch() and no parallel fetch, prefetch plus core 4.8 ms), ApplyBatch 4.2 against a 1.2 ms flush (about 0.5 us a change, records included), confirm 0.09.
- The node at the tip: a client from a snapshot one block below, a test framework peer delivering each block as a compact block after its transactions went to the mempool (all, 80%, 50%; the rest by getblocktxn), or as a whole block with an empty mempool; latency from the send (serialized before the clock starts) to getbestblockhash; scripts checked (-assumevalid=0), 8 cores, -dbcache=1024. Chain DX: 30 heavy blocks from 3,500 (1.13 MB, 3,000 transactions), 30 empty blocks from 5,000 (the only other kind chain DX has). Mainnet: blocks 240,000 to 240,029 from the local serving data (227 transactions on average), pruned snapshots, whole blocks only (no mocktime on mainnet, and a 2013 block time stops the client taking an unsolicited compact block).
- Before: heavy full mempool equal, 80% equal, 50% 3% slower, whole block 5.5% slower (293 against 278 ms with the old timing). Cause, by probes: ApplyChanges 19.5 ms at the tip, nearly all of it the apply reading the database again for every spend of an input the tip view had read without keeping it (CoinsStore::ApplySpend(), serial, under cs_main); then ActiveTipChange waiting 5.9 ms under cs_main for the transaction download lock, held by net processing's BlockConnected for the same block (its signal handed on just before, in NotifyTipAdvanced()), with the tip unpublished; then 2.7 ms after UpdateTip waiting for the block's undo write, queued only after the apply.
- Three commits on the series, 28 to 30 (tip 059be58f5a, backup `utxo-partial-order-before-tipfixes`): 9289b6ba19 ActiveTipChange before the pass's signals are handed on; 876afdbe01 a block connected alone queues its undo write before its apply; 059be58f5a a block connected alone takes the coins its apply misses from its undo data (in the batch, looked up only on a miss). A first form of the last, keeping the tip's database reads in the store, was faster (whole block 115 against 119 ms) but broke master's `connect_tip_does_not_cache_inputs_on_failed_connect` and was dropped; a revision that looked the undo coin up for every spend made the full-mempool case 6% slower and was corrected before the commit. Unit suite 397/397; functional subset (`gate_tip.sh`: notifications, mempool around blocks, transaction download, orphans, wallet, REST, mining, feature_block, compact blocks, invalid and unrequested blocks, the window and lag tests) all passed. IBD unchanged: chain DX -reindex-chainstate 301.1 to 307.4 against 301.3 to 315.9 blocks/s, syncs 232.2 / 225.7 against 212.7 / 231.0.
- After (median ms, three alternating rounds unless said): heavy full mempool 52.1 / 52.8 / 52.3 against 51.6 / 51.4 / 52.6 (equal); heavy 80% 91.0 / 93.4 against 93.4 / 94.3 (two rounds; with the first form 89.2 / 91.0 / 89.4 against 93.1 / 95.3 / 95.5, beyond the spread); heavy 50% 157.0 / 155.3 / 161.1 against 161.0 / 161.3 / 160.1 (first form; faster by median, one round overlaps); heavy whole block 120.4 / 118.8 / 118.5 against 121.4 / 121.1 / 119.2 (equal, about 1.5 ms lower); empty compact blocks about 0.3 ms above the baseline (8.3 against 7.9, ranges overlap); mainnet whole block, cold 16.7 / 17.1 / 18.7 against 16.6 / 17.0 / 16.3 (equal, trend +0.5 ms); mainnet whole block after the mempool took what policy accepts (about a quarter) about equal (first form).
- Where the rest is (whole heavy block, bench lines): the core 4 ms above master (the parallel fetch, lever 2 above), apply and confirm 11.9 against master's 7.9 ms flush (per-change cost and records, lever 1); the undo write and the flush are where the series saves. Not reached: a whole block faster than the baseline beyond the spread. Candidates beyond the two levers, discussed with the maintainer and not built: the apply by shard on the connect pool's workers (ApplyBatch already groups by shard), and validating a large block's transactions in chunks as neighbouring window blocks (consensus aggregation: fees, sigops, coinbase, order of conflicts, undo order).
- Steady state broken down, 20 November (clean build; `timeline2.py`, probe binaries never on the series). Heavy block, full mempool, receipt to UpdateTip about 50 ms on both: receipt to reconstruction about 5 ms, reconstruction to the connect's start 16.9 (baseline) and 18.9 ms (series), the connect about 27 ms (core 8.4, mempool cleanup in postprocess about 11, no script queue work at all: Verify equals Connect). Inside the stretch before the connect (series): CheckBlock 2.1, the three acceptance holds about 3, the block write 4.0, the hand-off 0.2, and NewPoWValidBlock 8.4 ms under cs_main, of which 7.2 ms replacing net processing's most recent block map, whose destruction frees the previous block's transactions (out of the mempool since it was connected). Master pays the same. Empty blocks: 2.6 + 2.5 + 0.2 ms on both, equal; the series has no fixed extra cost per block from receipt to tip (the 0.3 ms of the end-to-end runs were noise or after UpdateTip).
- Commit 32, c600e4b1f9: the previous block, its compact form and its map are taken out under the lock as before and freed on a worker of the connect pool. Not called in initial block download. Unit suite, the tip subset and the receive-path subset (p2p_compactblocks among them) passed. Against the baseline (commit 31 included, three rounds each): heavy full mempool 42.9 / 43.2 / 42.6 against 52.1 / 51.9 / 51.3 (about 17% faster), 80% 83.9 / 83.2 / 82.5 against 92.6 / 92.5 / 93.6 (about 10%), 50% 154.8 / 154.1 / 152.1 against 159.8 / 158.2 / 159.5 (about 3.5%), all beyond the spread. - Window width above the default, 20 November: the maintainer raised MAX_CONNECT_WINDOW (validation.h; the hard ceiling is 99, COINBASE_MATURITY, static_assert in ConnectWindow()) and ran a mainnet -reindex-chainstate on their machine with a wider window (-connectwindow above 64 at 32 workers, dbcache 30000): no change in runtime (figure not given). The window width is not a limit there; the earlier reading (finished blocks rarely wait behind the lowest, 7.4) stands. Not to be raised again.
- The store's apply, broken down by a sampled probe (20 November, clean build, `-reindex-chainstate` of chain DX at 8 cores, 19.8 million changes, every 8th timed; probe never on the series, `probe_apply2.py` in the job tmp; steady_clock costs about 20 ns here, so each component reads about 20 ns high): per change of the whole batch 446 ns: prefetch phase (PrefetchAll and the neighbour prefetch with its own find per spend) 95, the record's insertion into the block's record under its mutex 46, the per-change loop about 300. In the loop, as read: map find 23 (about the timer floor: warm after the prefetch), insert (try_emplace, node from the depot) 73, list detach 24 and attach 25 and state transition 21 (each at the floor), undo push 38; the rest, about 240 ns of the sampled 462, is not attributed (probably the accounting atomics, Resized(), See(), the coin move; not probed). Reading: records, list maintenance and transitions together are 25 to 40 ns of 446 (under 10%), so lever 1 and lazy age lists (discussed 20 November) can save well under 10% of the apply, under 2% of worker CPU: not worth their risk. The visible costs are the memory latency of the first touch (the prefetch phase, 21%) and the insert (about 11%), which a spend-as-mark store has too. Next probe if wanted: the accounting atomics.

The harness cannot show a disk wait: chain DX's 2.5 GB sit in the page cache, so a read there is a copy, and the gain at 12 workers is that copy made on a spare core. No consensus difference and no high finding. Open, most severe first. (1) Plausible, crash safety: `CollectRecords()` (`coinsstore.cpp:1698-1717`) scans the open blocks' records one slot mutex at a time with no shard held, and a takeover in `SpendLocked()` (`:975-1003`, item marked dead in the higher block, the copy appended to the lower block's record later through `Record()`) between two slot scans hides a confirmed dirty coin from the run; the run then settles the database above the coin's height without it, and a crash before the next run loses it. Needs an invalid block with valid proof of work in the window that spends an outpoint a lower open block spends, the interleaving, and the crash; the same hole in `Flush()` with blocks open. Not traced on a run. (2) A pending pong waits on the global hand-off mark (`net_processing.cpp:5632-5636`, `5868-5878`), not on the peer's own blocks as its comment says: a peer that serves no blocks stalls behind a connector pass of other peers' blocks, and none of its messages is taken meanwhile. (3) `StoredCoinData` mixes `uint32_t : 31` with `bool : 1` under `static_assert(sizeof(StoredCoin) == 48)` (`coinsstore.h:130-131`): MSVC does not pack the two, so the assert would fail there; not compiled. (4) `EmplaceCoinInternalDANGER()` (`coinsstore.cpp:651,658`) books the Coin's usage, not the StoredCoin's: snapshot coins with scripts of 36 bytes and more are under-booked and `SanityCheck()`'s total can disagree. (5) The undo-write effect calls `WriteBlockUndoAt()` bare on the effects thread (`validation.cpp:3236`, `effects.cpp:139`): a write failure throws `std::ios_base::failure` there and terminates, past the effect's own failure path, which only an open or close failure reaches; the write-out effect beside it uses `util::RunWorkerTask`. (6) `m_downloading_since` is not restarted while a handed-off entry is still in `vBlocksInFlight` (`net_processing.cpp:1474`). (7) The window byte account is one per node (`validation.cpp:4615, 5038`); two chainstates in window steps at once would overwrite each other's figure; whether they can run at once was not established. (8) A create over a dirty confirmed coin records `dirty` but not `fresh` (`coinsstore.cpp:919-922`), so an unwind costs one unneeded write and erase; only at the two BIP30 duplicates. (9) `FinishAcceptBlock()` leaves `*fNewBlock` true when the entry failed during the write. Re-cut debris, intermediate commits only: `ReadBlock()` catches `std::exception` at commits 1 to 12 (narrowed in 13); four code lines inside the docstring of `ConnectTip()` at commits 6 to 12, `block_pos`/`known_bytes` added in 6 and unused until 13; a doubled docstring paragraph in commit 5; comment fragments in 7 and 13 to 19; comments in 3, 8, 9, 11, 13 that name things of later commits.
