11 prune events and 528 to 529 MiB in every run, foreign load 0.5 to 0.7 cores; the series' runs at 8 and 14 cores spread wider than at 1024 (11% and 16%). Client CPU per run 58 to 84 s against the baseline's 142 to 148 s; peak RSS 375 to 611 MiB against 309 to 327 MiB (the baseline's cache is emptied at each prune event's flush). Pruning costs the series as much as the baseline at 4 cores and more at 8 and 14, as at 1024 before commit 15 took most of it; what holds `cs_main` at 450 was not sampled. Hypothesis, untested (left on the maintainer's word, 19 November: pruning is fine for now): at 450 memory runs are frequent, and a flush that unlinks pruned files waits under `cs_main` for a memory run it starts (`|| fFlushForPrune` in the lag path), the collision the removed fourth part of commit 15 avoided, measured only at 1024. Test: probe F's `settle-lag-run-pruning` count on one pruned 8-core run at 450, then the tip against the tip with that part restored, interleaved. At 30 the series is 1.9x to 2.9x the baseline in syncs at every core count, 2 cores included (22.9 against 12.3 blocks/s, client CPU 154 against 220 s), so the floor holds.

A pruned mainnet IBD on the harness host (19 November, on the maintainer's go; an end-to-end check, not a performance figure): the tip 343b027776 (`cpu/bin-prune`), `-prune=20000 -par=16 -prevoutfetchthreads=16 -stopatheight=975073 -disablewallet`, 16 cores (Neoverse-N1, no SMT), 30.5 GiB RAM, a QEMU virtual disk (Hetzner vServer), about ten peers on v2. Heights 0 to 856,353 with `-dbcache=16000` in 1:35:22 (25,322 s user, 6,154 s system, peak RSS 21.1 GB), then resumed on the same datadir with `-dbcache=6000` to 975,073 in 0:53:04 (19,580 s user, 3,136 s system, 20.8 GB): 2:28:26 in all, a clean stop, no warning but the absent Tor control port. A first attempt with `-prune=550` was stopped at about height 375,000 and deleted on the maintainer's word: at that target every prune event forces a write-out to a few hundred blocks below the tip, and the single write-out executor ran saturated. What set the pace, measured during the run: the peers (every request slot full, two of nine peers delivering 60%) to about height 450,000; the network thread's v2 decryption (92% of a core at 213 MB/s) and the prune events (master's IBD buffer prunes to just below the tip, so each event writes out most of the cache and the connector waits for it, about a minute at a time) to about 830,000; then disk reads. With the 16000 cache only about 6.7 GB of page cache was left for a 13 GB chainstate, the page cache thrashed (700 MB/s read at 11,500 reads/s for about 30 MB/s of blocks) and the rate fell to 8.6 blocks/s; at 6000 the reads fell to 1.6 MB/s and the rate rose to about 78, until the 2024-2025 blocks took the disk to its random-read limit (34,100 reads/s at about 9 KB, 100% busy) at about 25 blocks/s. Nothing in it points at the series: master with the same cache meets the same page cache and disk limits. Open, not pursued: prune events sized by need instead of master's IBD buffer, and runs overlapping publication (not simple, 4.4), would shorten the prune stalls (4.10, 5.2).

Take-aways from this run (the analysis of 19 November; measured where a figure is given, reasoning otherwise):

- **What it shows.** The series syncs a pruned node through the whole chain, the 2023 to 2025 blocks included, with a clean stop and no fault. In absolute terms it was fast for the hardware: about 700 GB in 2:28, about 80 MB/s on average, at the network thread's v2 ceiling (about 210 MB/s) in its good stretches. What it does not show: the series' share of that speed. There was no master run on the same host, and with real peers one would be blurred by peer luck as on signet; a ratio needs the maintainer's machine or a harness run.
- **The cache must leave the chainstate room in the page cache.** On disk the UTXO set is compact (13 GB here at 975,073); in the coins cache each coin takes a larger structure, so a page cache that holds the chainstate files covers more coins per GB than the coins cache. With `-dbcache=16000` on 30.5 GiB, about 6.7 GB of page cache was left, and misses became random disk reads at about 61 KB each (readahead, inferred): 700 MB/s read for about 30 MB/s of blocks, 8.6 blocks/s. At 6000 the reads fell to 1.6 MB/s. The rule of thumb that follows: leave the chainstate's size on disk, plus room for the block files the window reads back, in the page cache, and give the rest to `-dbcache`. Not series-specific; master with the same cache hits the same wall. The exact break-even point was not tested.
- **At a small prune target pruning becomes a continuous write-out.** The series deletes a file only below a coins head on disk (C5, D1), so at `-prune=550` every prune event forced a run to a few hundred blocks below the tip, about 200 write-out lines a minute, and the single write-out executor ran saturated while the connect workers idled (the first attempt, deleted). At 20 GB the events were rare but large.
- **Master's IBD prune buffer turns the bounded prune run into a whole-cache write.** During IBD `FindFilesToPrune` adds 1 MB per remaining block to its buffer, so each event prunes to just below the tip. That policy fits master, which flushes its whole cache at every prune event whatever it deletes; in the series a prune run writes only the changes at or below the height pruned to, so pruning by need (just enough to get a margin under the target) would write only the oldest changes per event. Spend ages (7.5: 79% of coins spent within about 144 blocks) suggest the total written would change little; the gain would be many short stalls instead of few long ones. Not built, not measured.
- **The stall is the connector's wait for the run, not the write itself.** A run started in the publication hold is waited for before the next block is published (the store's run must not see newly confirmed changes), so each prune event paused publication for about a minute from 2018 on. Pruning itself needs only that the files wait for the run (commit 15 already chooses them later). Letting runs overlap publication (5.2, the low-end lever) is the way to remove these stalls and the memory runs' alike, but it is not simple: 4.4 records that a run overlapping publication is wrong as such, and that the simple rule (cap the settled height below any dirty entry changed during the run) would hold the written height almost still, since published blocks spend coins of every age. A prune run would need its own form of it, a design question for the low-end phase.
- **The peers limit early on through window and round trip.** With every request slot full (130 to 155 blocks in flight), two peers with 25 and 114 ms pings delivered 60% of the data and peers with 0.4 to 2 s pings 4 to 29 MB/s each. A larger request window for peers that deliver quickly, beside the existing rule for slow ones, would be the receive-side lever; noted, not pursued.
- **v2 decryption on the single network thread binds on a real network too:** 92% of a core at 213 MB/s here, as in the harness (7.7).
- **`getpeerinfo` did not answer within 30 s under IBD load**, once; worth a look only if it recurs.
- **HDD setups (reasoning, untested).** The series' parallel coin reads across a window give a disk a deep queue it can reorder, the syncs left `cs_main`, and the 50% write-out share wrote 26% fewer changes than master's write-everything in the maintainer's runs of 16 November (12 runs against 9, the connector stopped 28.0 against 39.4 s); all of that should help on an HDD. The connector's wait for each run grows with disk latency and is the weak point there, a pruned HDD node with a small cache the worst case; the best kept share may sit below 50% on an HDD (each run's syncs cost more). The gain over master is expected to be real but smaller than on an SSD, since validation's spread over cores helps little against seeks. Settled only by a `-reindex-chainstate` with the chainstate on an HDD, series against master, and the share curve repeated there.

Which parts of the pruning waits (then commit 25) are needed (the same day, `cpu/c25_parts.sh`, log `cpu/c25-parts-2026-11-19.log`; builds from the tip with parts removed, interleaved, three rounds, pruned, `-dbcache=1024`, medians at 8 / 14 cores): the settle only at the target alone 174.3 / 193.9; with the deferred run but without `m_prune_at_periodic_write` 178.7 / 191.7 (the deferral does not take effect); with both 181.4 / 222.0; the full commit, which also started no memory run in a flush that prunes in the hold, 180.1 / 217.8. That fourth part was taken out: the commit (now 15, 3c3cbd520b) is the 181.4 / 222.0 build. At the large budget pruning now costs the series less than the baseline at 4 and 14 cores and more at 8 (26% against 17%); at the low budget about as much as before and as the baseline. 11 prune events and 528 to 529 MiB in all 24 runs. At the low budget the store writes out for memory throughout, so a prune event's run adds little.

**The harness sync's limits, in the order they bind** (summary of the paragraphs above, 18 November; chain DX, three local peers unless noted, the current code):

| # | Limit | Status | Binds at | Evidence | Way past |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | one master peer | removed with two or more peers | about 170 blocks/s, 8 cores | its message handler about 80% busy; two peers 191.6 against 169.9 (1.13x); three no better than two | two or more peers, a chain top under a day old on the harness |
| 2 | block-file sync under `cs_main` at a new block file (master's code) | fixed, the block-file trim (now commit 9) | about 210 blocks/s, 8 cores | holds up to 190 ms; probe C 221 to 254; the commit 209.9 to 238.0 (1.13x) | trim, the next index write syncs |
| 3 | v2 decryption on the one network thread | the limit with v2 | about 245 blocks/s from 8 cores, every large budget (about 280 MB/s) | the thread at 0.94 to 0.99 of a core, workers idle; the cipher 392 MB/s a core; v1 378.0 against 246.3 (1.53x) | a faster cipher (OpenSSL's 874 MB/s here), decryption spread over peers; not built |
| 4 | the client's cores | the limit with v1 | about 380 blocks/s at 14 cores (about 430 MB/s) | 12.3 of 14 cores, workers ready without a core; about 32 ms of core time a heavy block | more cores, cheaper work per block |
| 5 | the effects thread (undo writes, runs) | next serial limit, projected | about 520 blocks/s | 0.73 of a core at 384 blocks/s | not examined |
| 6 | the network thread without decryption (v1) | projected | about 670 blocks/s | 0.57 of a core at 384 blocks/s | socket reading over more threads |
| 7 | the connector, `cs_main` contention | not near at these rates | not measured above about 400 | the connector waits for workers | watch at higher rates |
| | `-dbcache=30 -maxmempool=5` | separate plateau | about 43 blocks/s from 8 cores (2.9x master) | flat from 8 to 14 cores | the low-end path (5.2) |

Ruled out on the way: disk waits (about 0%), the serving side (spare capacity), the receive-side `cs_main` look-ups (probe A, no change). Master syncs at about 22 blocks/s at every core count. Rows 5 and 6 are projections from loads measured at one rate.

### 7.9 The memory profile of 2026-11-18 (the maintainer's machine, the machines series at 74e8e1db9a)

Two collections with `/work/patches/utxo-partial-order-memprofile.sh` on a `-reindex-chainstate` run (16 cores, 32 threads, `-dbcache=30000 -prevoutfetchthreads=32`), sampled late in the run for ten seconds: one on glibc (at height 841,015), one with jemalloc preloaded (at height 856,366). The preload is a measuring aid only; no allocator swap goes on the series (section 2), and the run time with it is not recorded on the maintainer's word. The two samples are 15,000 blocks apart, so small differences between them mean nothing. `-par` differed between the runs and is irrelevant below assumevalid.

Readings:

- The processors are fully occupied: all 32 connect workers carry about the same share. The run is memory-bound: 0.55 instructions per cycle on glibc, 0.65 with jemalloc, against 2.26 measured above assumevalid.
- The allocator is about 23% of CPU on glibc (frees about 17 points, mostly `_int_free_merge_chunk`) and about 3.4% with jemalloc. Instructions in ten seconds rose from 766 to 898 billion, so removing the allocator's cost altogether is worth about 1.09x: the ceiling for allocation work in code. CPU saved converts at about half, the rest is absorbed by the store's loads. The call stacks stop at the libc boundary, so the allocating call sites are not identified.
- The coin hash (`SaltedCoinsCacheHasher`) is 11 to 12% of CPU in both runs, pure computation. `memset` 2 to 3%, `SerializeTransaction<SizeComputer>` 2 to 3%, `_copy_to_iter` 1.6 to 2.6%.
- Cache misses are the same in both runs (6.4 billion in ten seconds) although everything else changed.
- Far loads (c2c header, jemalloc run): of the loads that leave a core's own caches, 66% are served from main memory, 18% from a line modified in another core's cache, 17% from the shared L3. The table of contended lines came out empty.
- Load latency by source line (jemalloc run): about 32% of the weight sits on the store's prefetch instructions (`Map::Ahead()`: 16.1% in the `ApplyBatch()` batch, 11.1% in `FetchFor()`, 4.7% the write prefetch of list neighbours) and about 3% on the probes that follow them (the key compare in `find()` inside `ApplyBatch()` is 0.14%). The prefetches are timely: neither late nor missing.
- The other waits are scattered: the block-file copy out of the page cache 19.4% in many short loads; `ConfirmBlock()` reading the change record other workers wrote 4.8%; `BlockApplyView::Prefetch()` collecting outpoints 4.1%; `ApplyBatch()` outside the batch 6.8%; `AnnounceCreates()` about 2.8%; `ApplyChanges()` 1.5%; `BlockMerkleRoot()` 1.3%. No single large target.

Open, not verified:

- Settled 2026-11-19 from the same two collections' CPU recordings (perf record, cycles, 5 s): the two batch lambdas carry 19.9% and 13.1% of the load-latency weight on glibc but 1.98% and 1.67% of the cycles, and 20.9% and 11.9% against 3.87% and 3.42% with jemalloc. A thread waiting on a load accrues cycle samples there, so the weight on the prefetch lines is their fill latency, overlapped, not waiting: the store's misses are hidden. The cycles go to computation and many small items instead (the coin hash 11.6%, `BlockApplyView::ApplyChanges()` 5.2%, SHA-256 3.6%, `memset` 3.1%, frees 2.8%, the kernel's block-file copy 2.6%, jemalloc collection).
- Whether 32 threads saturate the memory system: the maintainer's runs of 18 November with 16 and 24 connect threads (7.1) answer as far as one machine can: 12:27.55 and 11:46.05 against 11:36.26 at 32, so the threads beyond 16 buy 7% of wall time for 1.79x the CPU time, and those beyond 24 1.4%. The processor has 16 cores and 32 hardware threads, so the result does not separate the memory system from a core's shared resources (two workers per core above 16); either way the parallelism past one worker per core is nearly spent there, and the remaining time is the work per coin. (These runs were given on 18 November and recorded only on 19 November; until then this point read: not answerable from these collections, both at 32 threads.) A rough bound: 6.4 billion cache misses in 10 s, about 41 GB/s if each is a 64-byte line, a large share of dual-channel DDR5 in practice but not clearly its limit (the counter's meaning on this processor and the write traffic not checked). A run at `-prevoutfetchthreads=16` answers it.
- Why a prefetch weighs ten to fourteen times a block-copy load. Either the memory system is saturated by 32 threads (which would also explain the equal miss counts, and harness gains that ran flat on this machine), or a core queues the burst `PrefetchAll()` issues behind its limit of outstanding misses, which is harmless while the probes arrive after the data. A run with `-prevoutfetchthreads=16` tests the first; a flat result does not tell a per-core from a whole-system limit, since two hardware threads share a core's miss capacity.

Candidates named, none measured or decided: a cheaper coin hash (the upstream SipHash work is to be read first), the block read's zero-fill and copy, the second walk over transactions for their size. Pacing the prefetches is not indicated by this evidence.

### 7.10 The young table measured (the maintainer's machine, 2026-11-18)

The design is in 4.12. The maintainer's runs, `-reindex-chainstate -stopatheight=975073 -dbcache=30000 -par=16 -prevoutfetchthreads=32 -networkactive=0 -disablewallet -debug=coindb`, at tip 7abcf2575f (the series at 74e8e1db9a plus the two hint commits, which do nothing in this run, plus the two young table commits):

| `-coinsyoungtable` | wall | user | system | CPU | against 11:36.26 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1024 | 12:28.89 | 18270.93 s | 1219.59 s | 2602% | 0.930x |
| 4096 | 12:16.98 | 18353.60 s | 1211.83 s | 2654% | 0.945x |

The reference is the maintainer's earlier run of the tree of 74e8e1db9a, 11:36.26 (7.1: 17381.58 s user, 1182.99 s system); its exact options are not recorded. One run for each value. The counter lines of the two logs (ended there, lookups answered) were not read, so which share of the created coins the table kept from the main table is not known.

The reading: the table costs 6% to 7% of wall time on the full-memory run. The expectation before the run, 1.03x to 1.10x, was arithmetic on the spend ages of 7.5 and was wrong. Causes named, none measured:

- The misses it removes were hidden already. A coin spent one to six blocks after its creation had its bucket and its node touched a fraction of a second before, and the create's own probe was among the batch's overlapped prefetches.
- Every lookup pays one more probe, the lookups of old coins included, in 8 MiB (1024) or 32 MiB (4096) of slot arrays that thirty-two workers reach in no order.
- The prefetch pass of a batch reads the young slot of every item, twice, to decide what to ask for: loads that wait where before it issued prefetches only.
- A full table is moved on in one piece with the shard held, and a coin that is moved on has paid for both tables.
- A lookup counts itself in the map's header: a store on a path that only read.

### 7.11 The perf collection of 2026-11-22 (the maintainer's machine, tip cbef835ea5)

Collected with `/work/parallel-validation/perf-collect-2026-11-22.sh` during a `-reindex-chainstate` run with the usual options (9950X3D, kernel 7.0, perf 7.0.14); the text reports are in `/work/reports.tar.gz`. Collection A at 303 to 345 s of the run, collection B at 482 to 521 s. The run crossed into script checking at about 497 to 500 s: B's counters and flat profile (482 to 499 s) are still below assumevalid, B's call graphs and later steps are above it (71% of CPU in `CheckInputScripts`, 55% in `secp256k1_ecdsa_verify`). On the maintainer's word A is the reading that counts. The run's wall time is no timing. The scheduler-switch stacks and the system-call summaries were collected but not read out.

Readings from A (shares of CPU cycles unless said otherwise):

- Occupation: 28.9 of 32 hardware threads busy over ten seconds. A connect worker is on a CPU 89.5% of the time, waits for one 4.1% and sleeps 6.4% (B: 91.0, 4.6, 4.4). The connector thread (`b-initload`) is on a CPU 9% and waits for one 33% of the time. 0.62 instructions per cycle (B's counters 0.65).
- The allocator is about 25%: `_int_free_chunk` 11.8, `__libc_malloc2` 8.9, `_int_malloc` 1.7, the rest 2.3 (B's flat profile, later blocks: 14.5, 10.7, 2.9). The call sites are identified for the first time, and nearly all of it is the parsed block:
  - freeing a block (`BlockBins::Bin::Empty()`, on the thread that built it) 13.5% inclusive: `~vector<CTxIn>` 8.6 (the witness stacks), `CTransaction` 3.8;
  - allocating while parsing (`ReadBlock()`): malloc self time 7.3, of which witness items and stacks 4.7, the input vectors 1.2; witness deserialisation 6.1 inclusive.
  - So the witness stacks are about 14 to 15% of CPU here (6.1 to build, 8.6 to free). This is the subject of the separate witness pull request (not for the series).
- `ReadBlock()` 23.5% inclusive (parsing 17.9, hashing 5.2, the file read 5.6 with `_copy_to_iter` 2.9 and `kernel_init_pages` 1.3 for page cache pages); `ConnectBlock()` 25.4; `ApplyChanges()` 18.4; the continuation 15.3 (`FinishDeferredTransactions()` 11.6).
- The inputs of deferred transactions are read one at a time: `CoinsStore::LookupFor()` 7.7% inclusive, and it carries most of `pthread_mutex_unlock` and `pthread_mutex_trylock` (3.4 and 2.2 in all). The batched fetch for the continuation was commit 48 on 14 November and was dropped for no gain on mainnet (5.4); the share is recorded here, the ruling stands.
- The view's own table: `HaveCoinInCache()` from `ConnectBlock()` 5.0 inclusive, `FetchCoin()` 11.8 inclusive, `SaltedCoinsCacheHasher` 3.4 (11 to 12% on 18 November), `AddCoins()` 4.7.
- Sweep candidates with a share now: S2 `SerializeTransaction<SizeComputer>` 2.5 (3.7 in B's flat profile); sigops `GetSigOpCount` 1.7; `CheckBlock()` 5.8 inclusive (S4); S1 `UpdateCoins()` 2.0 inclusive plus `SettleUndo()` 0.9, an upper bound, since the spend itself is inside; S3 is inside the witness share. S5: workers sleep 6.4%, causes not read out. S6, S7: the load-latency weight sits on the two batch lambdas as on 18 November (26.0% and 14.4%, main memory), which 7.9 reads as prefetch fill latency; nothing new. S8: `_int_free_merge_chunk` is 0.7 here, the trim path does not show.
- Load latency: 78% of the weight is served from main memory; `_copy_to_iter` 23.5% of the weight.
- Read out later the same day. System calls of the connect workers over 3 s of A (taken under `perf trace`, which slows every call, so the times are inflated and only the counts are used): about 4,700 futex calls a second per worker, a third of them returning at once because the lock word had changed; 1,550 sched_yield a second (the shard lock's try-then-yield); 67 reads a second. The untraced counters give 1,548 voluntary switches a second per worker for 6.4% asleep, about 41 microseconds a sleep: short lock waits, not waits for work. The switch stacks are cut at 2% and name one cause only: `SettleUndo()` freeing the undo data (built by the first pass's worker, freed by the continuation's) and sleeping on glibc's arena lock inside `_int_free_chunk`. One sample; which locks carry the rest is not known.

- Read out in full, 22 November, from the same reports (the per-thread switch report keeps the frames above the cut, so the shares below are sums at named frames; nothing run, nothing built; the collection is from before the plain shard mutex). No switch of a worker is a wait for a task: every one is inside a block. A worker's time, untraced: A on a CPU 89.5%, runnable without a CPU 4.1%, asleep 6.4% (1,548 voluntary switches a second, 41 microseconds each); B 91.0%, 4.6%, 4.4% (2,204 a second, 20 microseconds). The connector: on a CPU 8.9% and 7.7%, runnable without one 32.6% and 37.4%. The workers' switches in A by cause, as shares of their count: the block file read in `ReadBlock()` 27% (half of it seen down to the kernel's read); the arena lock in a free 33%, of which the undo data dropped in `SettleUndo()` 24% (a -reindex-chainstate writes no undo, so this place is the reindex's alone, S1) and the previous block freed in `BlockBins::NewBlock()` 8%; the shard lock in `LookupFor()` 16%, half of it the yield of the retry lock that is gone since; malloc under 2%; the rest under the report's cut. In the three seconds of B's switch record the process switched a tenth as often, and the workers' switches were the block file read (28%) and timer preemption inside the script checks; no lock. By time the split is not measured: `perf trace` gives 144 ms of read calls per worker in 3 s of A (4.8% of its time, the copy on the CPU included) and 17 ms in B, and futex times that tracing inflates beyond use. If most of the read time is a wait, the reads are the larger part of the sleep and the locks are under 2% of a worker's time. Which thread holds the arena lock a free waits for is not in the data.
- S5 is closed, and was closed before the sweep listed it: the maintainer's run of 20 November with a wider window showed no change (5.4, "Window width above the default"). The sweep should not have carried it.

## 8. Dropped, withdrawn, and the simplification review

### 8.1 Tried or weighed and dropped

The reason stands where the subject is; this list exists so that none is proposed again unknowingly.

- The pool per shard and the kept-node bound of 8192; the duplicate `HaveInputs` (7.3; built all the same on 16 November as F18 of performance-suggestions.md, on the maintainer's go, the 0.54% is the size to expect).
- Notifying the connector only for the lowest block; one bin shared by all threads; a freeing thread, a preallocated region, another allocator (4.3).
- Master's pool allocator for the tier (11% more coins for the same bytes), not built: master's pool returns memory only when destroyed, the rebuild would have to be driven by the budget, and the chunk size chosen so the shards do not overshoot a small budget.
- Overlapping lag runs with publication (4.4).
- 1024 shards as the remedy for the flush stalls (4.8).
- A rule that opened the window below the BIP34 height, and the round for a run below 20 minutes with its instrumentation (7.1); the height clause itself went later, for a different reason (4.6).
- A read-ahead hint for block bytes (7.1); skipping the wtxid for a block read from disk (7.4).
- The young table, a small table for each shard for the coins blocks create (4.12): 0.930x and 0.945x on the maintainer's full-memory reindex run (7.10), dropped on 2026-11-18, kept on branch `utxo-partial-order-young-table-trial`.
- One counted byte budget for parsed blocks; removing the effects thread, in the hold or on the connector; a block cache default of an eighth of the coins cache (7.7).
- The hash pool and `DeserializeBlock()`, on the series as commit 13 and removed by commit 43 (4.7).
- A write-out run at every prune check (4.10).
- A mainnet snapshot for the network work (4.7); asking one peer for more than sixteen blocks; a sync from signet (7.5).

Parked or held and not on the series are in 5.4.

### 8.2 Withdrawn readings

Claimed once and taken back; not to be claimed again.

- "The publisher at 60% of a core" and the bound of 1.6x derived from it.
- A window worker spends three to four times the CPU per transaction of the serial path: the serial figure was the connector's wall time while pool workers prefetch its inputs (`StagedConnect::FetchGuard`), not a thread's CPU (7.3 has the measurement).
- 1.3x for the sweep's removal (1.10x measured, 7.1).
- The idle time read by the length of the sleeps; it follows their number and kind (7.1, 7.4). Page faults from the allocator's growth as the cause of the extra blocking (a micro-test gives 1 to 2 points, not 7 to 12); the cause was the allocator's lock (7.4).
- The benchcoin readings before the artifact, "likeliest the peer" among them, and the write-out at the prune ceiling as the bound (7.5).
- Backwards scaling past eight workers: the BIP30 check on regtest (6.3). The plateau past ten workers at `-dbcache=450`: the window's byte cap (4.5).
- The weighting of the receive side against the store by lines, corrected by the ruling in section 1.
- "That simple fix" for the flush meant four flush threads; my reading of it as the stall fix alone was wrong. The maintainer: "You should ask back if you are not sure what I mean."

### 8.3 The two defects as found

Both closed (4.5, 4.6); kept for the mechanism.

- The BIP30 gap. A window block's BIP30 check did not see a coin that was never written and that a higher open block had spent, because the spend erased the entry at apply and an applier's miss does not scan the open blocks' records. It can only matter where BIP30 is enforced inside the window and a duplicate transaction can be built: a mainnet fork without the BIP34 block above height 227,931, regtest with `-testactivationheight=bip34@N` for N above 1, and mainnet from height 1,983,702; not on the real mainnet chain today.
- Exceptions on the pool. `RunContinuation` caught `std::logic_error` and `std::runtime_error` and had no guard like `RunConnectTask`'s. `ThreadPool::Submit` wraps a task in a packaged task, so any other exception was stored in a future nobody reads, `finished` was never set and the connecting thread waited for ever; the same wrapping meant an allocation failure on a pool worker did not terminate the node.

### 8.4 Job 2, the simplification review of 2026-11-11

Two fresh read-only reviewers; every finding checked against the code. The full record (instructions, reports, both rounds) is in `/work/utxo-partial-order-job2/`; here the outcome of each item. **Closed on 21 November 2026: nothing of job 2 is open.** The remaining points were checked by reading at tip 3c224b4d8b (`/work/parallel-validation/full-r13-verified.md`, section L) and the maintainer had them marked resolved.

- Folded on the maintainer's word ("fold in the ones that seem without a downside"): three dead window-block branches, `m_window_mutex`, the executor's tickets, an unreachable flush wait, `Shard::coins_usage`.
- The effects executor has no user of its own: the lag run, the undo write, and three completions that queue only to keep their order behind those two. It can go only if the lag run moves inline on the connecting thread (holds by trace, needs a small-cache baseline) and the undo is written inside the publishing hold, which is `cs_main` with the mempool mutex: about 1.35 ms for a heavy block in one harness sample against a block period of 1.67 ms on reindex, shown only by a full `-reindex` and a sync. With only one of the two it stays. The maintainer doubted the removal ("isnt the undo write kind of a big thing?"), and the runs bear that out: 0.82x on a network sync with both in the hold, 0.88x with both on the connector between blocks (7.7). It stays.
- M1, one final write path: `Flush()` as `Sync()` then `EvictClean(0)` then a rehash; the wipe, its helper thread, `m_marker_mutex` and one copy of the owed-erase rule would go. Its cost only a run with a 30 GB tier tells (5.2). **Resolved:** closed by the maintainer on 17 November (the parallel flush stays); `m_marker_mutex` has since gone from the tree.
- The block-index batch generation guard (`m_index_commit_mutex`, two counters) is not proven dead with two chainstates (assumeutxo), where the other chainstate's synchronous `WriteBlockIndexDB()` may commit a newer batch while the first's is queued. A possible latent defect either way, not verified by a run. **Resolved, 21 November:** neither dead nor a defect. The mutex and counters are now an assertion of the commit order. The effects executor belongs to the one `BlockManager` both chainstates share; a run's batch is prepared and queued under `cs_main` and committed in queue order, and every synchronous index write of either chainstate waits for that queue (`SettleWriteOut()`) before it prepares its own. It stays.
- Smaller removals proposed and undecided: `PrepareWriteOut` as test-only, `m_tier_height` folded into `m_confirmed_height`, `m_defer_write_out_wait` as an argument, an enum for a window block's progress bools; the block-index states (5.1); `-connectwindow` as a knob (the maintainer's call); acceptance in three holds of `cs_main`, the try-lock pass of `VisitShards` and front submission in the thread pool (one run each); T3 (5.2). **Resolved, 21 November:** `PrepareWriteOut` no longer exists; the progress bools became the `WindowBlockState` machine; the block-index states were dropped; `-connectwindow` is debug-only; `m_tier_height` and `m_defer_write_out_wait` are cosmetic and stay as they are; the three mechanisms a run would decide (three holds, the try-lock pass, front submission) stay as built, no run made and none planned; T3 was recorded here as taken (the plain shard mutex), which was wrong: the retry lock (`CoinsStore::ShardLock`, 20 microseconds) is in the code at fb1255d0f0 and was in every tip since; corrected 22 November, when the removal was cut as the trial branch `utxo-partial-order-plain-shard-mutex` for a run on the maintainer's machine (5.2). The thread pool exception defect seen during job 2 is closed: every task whose future is dropped is `noexcept` or only frees memory.
- Kept as essential: the retry rule, the script-execution-cache lock, deferral and continuations, the connector thread, the striped totals; and everything the low-end rule protects (dirty spans, clean lists and `EvictClean`, the lag runs, the owed erase, `CollectRecords`, `-test=write_out_every_block`).

## 9. Validation, export and the series

### 9.1 Export

The export is always `/work/patches/utxo-partial-order.mbox`, the whole series from the master commit the cut stands on (for the current cut `git format-patch --stdout e211dd0186..<tip>`, for the first cut from 6f1365e34f), checked for attribution and private strings, then sent. Commits carry no AI trailers. Nothing is pushed. The export of the 44 commits at 26bd9d762b, applied onto 6f1365e34f, gives the branch's tree.

### 9.2 What was validated

At the first built state (2026-11-10, before most of the series): full unit and functional suites, `feature_dbcrash.py` and `feature_pruning.py` against snapshot binaries.

The rebase onto master 6f1365e34f: no conflict over 60 master commits. The leveldb subtree moved (#36779); nothing was measured against it.

The ThreadSanitizer pass at delivery (build-tsan, upstream's CI options, 2026-11-13, before the reorder of 9.3). No data race and no lock-order report. Two findings, both fixed by folds:

1. A forced flush stopped the sanitizer itself: a flush worker keeps every shard a batch took changes from locked until the batch is written, and with shards of few changes one worker held its whole range of the 256, past the deadlock detector's bound on mutexes held by one thread; six unit suites hung inside the sanitizer. Fix in the young-tier commit: `MAX_BATCH_SHARDS{32}`, a worker ends its batch before it takes a 33rd shard.
2. `p2p_ibd_block_handoff.py` failed on any build since the request sizing commit (a new peer is asked for one block until its first arrived, the test waited for twelve requests). Fix: a fresh test peer delivers one block first (`deliver_first_block`).

Covered: the whole unit set under the sanitizer with full options (script_assets skipped for want of its data file), fifteen functional tests under the sanitizer including the series' own six and `p2p_ibd_stalling`. Not covered: `feature_pruning.py` to its end (its full-chain `verifychain` exceeds the 60 s RPC limit under the sanitizer, natively 1.9 s; the limit was not raised; the parts before ran without a report), `feature_dbcrash` (by rule), fuzz targets, bench, the kernel library, anything above 16 cores.

Pass 3 at its tip 82a1b90f76 (2026-11-14), after the fold of 4.11:

- Native (`build-clang`, all targets): no warning; the unit suite passes; the full functional suite against snapshot binaries, 517 entries: 493 passed, 23 skipped for what this build lacks (wallet migration and compatibility with old releases, IPC, USDT, ZMQ, GUI, the kernel tool), one failed. The failure is `feature_discover.py`, whose node could not bind 127.0.0.1:34092 at start; alone it passes. The runner's port base of 23000 puts the onion ports at 33000 and up, inside the kernel's range for outgoing connections, so another test's connection can hold the port; that reading was not confirmed, the failed node's log was deleted with the run's directory.
- ThreadSanitizer, the one pass (`build-tsan`, upstream's options from `test/with_sanitizer_env.py.in`): no warning in the build; all 366 unit entries pass (script_assets skipped for want of its data file) with no report; eighteen functional tests, `p2p_ibd_stalling.py` on both transports, pass with no report in any node's output: the series' own seven (`feature_block_cache`, `feature_async_undo`, `feature_connect_concurrency`, `feature_connect_window`, `feature_coins_lag`, `feature_coins_lag_prune`, `p2p_ibd_block_handoff`), and `p2p_compactblocks`, `feature_reindex`, `feature_assumeutxo`, `feature_block`, `feature_coinstatsindex`, `feature_init`, `mempool_reorg`, `p2p_segwit`, `rpc_invalidateblock`, `feature_fastprune`. `wallet_reorgsrestore.py` was skipped: the sanitizer build has no wallet.
- Not covered: `feature_dbcrash` and the extended functional tests (by rule), `feature_pruning.py` under the sanitizer, fuzz targets, the kernel library, the benches after the fold (the measurements of 7.7 are on the tree before it; the fold adds a wait in `PublishConnectTip()` outside initial block download only, and a predicate in the prune gate), anything above 16 cores.

### 9.3 The first cut, in order

The list below is the first cut as it stood at 50 commits; it grew to 51 before the hash pool was dropped (commits 13, 38 and 43 of this list went and the later ones moved up, 8.1); its final state is 48 commits, tip 2b58bf4cea (the branch `utxo-partial-order-v1`, deleted on 2026-11-22; `/work/patches/utxo-partial-order-v1.mbox`). Its order came from a reorder of 43 commits into 40 with an identical tree (91eb06f638), after which every commit was built for the first time; the states the branch passed through, review by review, are in the first-cut review record (4.11). Dropped from it and not to be proposed again: the block file read-ahead (commit 46 for one mainnet run, 13:49.90, no gain, 7.1) and commits 46 to 48 of 14 November (the continuation's batched fetch and input coins by position, 7.3; none showed a gain on the mainnet runs). The series does not touch `src/consensus`, `src/script` or `src/policy`.

### 9.4 The current cut, in order

Branch `utxo-partial-order`, tip b5fc79e546, 34 commits on master 19128e8b77, re-cut 2026-11-23 with the file work ahead of the store and the connector, all of it in commits 9 to 18; the tree is that of 82bda590a7, the 36 commits before the re-cut (the 34 below as they then stood, with the file space commit amended to 43f037bbb0, then 35 23a0b589a6 the reorganisation read-ahead and 36 82bda590a7 the check). What went where: the three-step split of `ProcessNewBlock()` (`AcceptNewBlock()`, `WriteNewBlock()`, `FinishNewBlock()`) from old 33 is in commit 9; commit 11 is new and holds what of old 34 and old 32 needs neither the executor nor the window: the allocation by the write, the claim that cannot fail, the trim deferred until no write is in flight, the block I/O threads with the removal of pruned files on one, `IsCursorFile()`, the `ReadBlockBytes()`/`ParseBlock()` split and the first text of the "File I/O" rule; the window commit 14 takes the window block's read on a block I/O thread (old 32) and what needs the executor (the queued trims, the undo claim and the allocation by a queued undo write, of old 34); the net processing commit 15 takes the receive task's three threads (old 33). The read-ahead (17) and the check (18) sit behind the page cache release (16): the read-ahead needs the window and the net processing commits (it conflicts before either), the check needs the read-ahead, one of its named exceptions, and `ReleaseBlockFilesBelow()` of commit 16, which it asserts in. They were first left last (tip 6a5419cc54) and moved up the same day by a clean replay; commits 19 to 34 touch neither the block storage files nor a call of a checked function, so the check says there what it says at the tip. The commits before the check read and write under cs_main in places their successors move, and none was run with it. The page cache release and the pruning waits (16 and 19) follow the window, where their call sites are. Commit 11 was written by hand on commit 10, the trees of commits 14 to 32 were recorded by reverting backwards from the tested tree and committed forwards, so an error in commit 11 would show as a stray reversal in commit 14's diff, which was read and has none. The tip was built once after each of the two re-cuts, and then commits 9 to 18 each at its place (on "just compile them, but don't run the tests there"), all without a warning; commits 19 to 33 were not built at their new places; no test was run on any of them, on the maintainer's word, the tree being the tested one. The rest of this parenthesis is the history of the cut before the re-cut: tip 85197a850d, 34 commits (commit 34 added 2026-11-23, the 33 below it unchanged, tip before 535ecd1492; commit 33 added and commit 32 reshaped onto the block manager's pool on 2026-11-22, commit 32 was 5a9cd4d355 before; commit 32 added 2026-11-22, the tip before it caec9dc0ce; rebased 2026-11-22 from e211dd0186, where the tip was b5ce5fd3de; 2026-11-22: the 34 commits of tip 3a957e1318 with the last three folded into commits 11, 13 and 14, the tree identical; of those three, 34 was the plain shard mutex, 33, fb1255d0f0, tests only, and the 32 below it the tip f80394c8f9: two comments folded into commits 11 and 14, previous tip a1747de620 on `utxo-partial-order-before-docs`; before it the cleanup pass folded, previous tip 7c165612cd on `utxo-partial-order-before-cleanup`; before it the fixes of review round 14 folded into their owners; same order and subjects as the re-cut of the same day, whose tip 9bfd5b7825 is kept on `utxo-partial-order-before-r14-fixes`; that re-cut put the refactors first and folded the last additions into the commits whose code they change, its tree identical to 274baabfc8, the 36 commits before it, on `utxo-partial-order-before-fold-recent`). The paragraph below describes the list as it stood on 2026-11-19.

Branch `utxo-partial-order`, tip 343b027776, 25 commits on master e211dd0186 (2026-11-19). The re-cut's 22 had tip 74e8e1db9a; the acceptance split (a block checked and written with cs_main released: three short holds, the being-stored record, pinned block files, the atomic check flags) was moved out of the window commit into commit 8, after the effects executor (backup `utxo-partial-order-before-accept-split` at 460a447cef). On 2026-11-19 the block-file trim, first on top, was moved to commit 9, after the acceptance split and before the store: it needs only commit 2's rule that the index write syncs every written file, its comment names `SyncWrittenFiles()` there, and the window commit adds the write-out run's index batch to the comment and a second test (`blockmanager_left_file_synced_by_the_index_batch`) beside the first (`..._synced_before_the_index_records_it`, a shared fixture). The page cache release (commit 14) and the pruning waits (commit 15) follow the window and net processing commits, the earliest place for each (the release's only call site is the window's connect task, the pruning waits change what the store and the window introduce). The tip's tree is the earlier tip's (3c3cbd520b, backup `utxo-partial-order-before-reorder`) but for the trim test. Commits 9, 12, 14 and 15 and the tip were built without a warning and passed the unit suite at their positions (389, 394, 396, 396, 397). Later the same day commit 15 clears `m_prune_at_periodic_write` through a scope guard on any way out of `PublishConnectTip()`, an exception included (backup `utxo-partial-order-before-guard`); the tip built and passed the unit suite and the pruning functional tests.

## 10. Sources

The sources something was taken from, and those compared and not taken. The first research round's own list, with links, is the "Sources" part of `/work/utxo-architecture-research.md`. "M" marks a source the maintainer gave.

- The Delving thread "Libbitcoin for Core people" (delvingbitcoin.org/t/libbitcoin-for-core-people/1222) (M): post 34, the link the maintainer gave, is evoskuil's (existence and spentness need the block order, script validation only a partial order); sipa's reply, post 36, has the "negative UTXO" entry that cancels when the output arrives. Together the origin of the window and deferral (section 1).
- Libbitcoin's v4 database design: validation split from confirmation.
- Hornet Node, UTXO(1): a store written by age at a steady rate with nothing emptied (the young tier and lag runs).
- purpleKarrot, "Towards a Side-Effect Free Validation Library", parts 1 to 3 (purplekarrot.net/blog/bitcoin-validation-library-3.html) (M): the pure core and the script cache as a sequential, a parallel and a sequential phase; recorded in `/work/validation-refactor-plan.md`.
- Bitcoin Core PRs: 31645 (`-dbbatchsize`) for the size of runs and flush batches; 31875 (sorted batches) for the key-ordered flush (4.8); 25325 (the pool resource for the coins cache) as the precedent behind the pool-per-shard trial (7.3).
- Benchcoin PR 201 with the artifact of run 42992592996 (M), and a friend's mainnet sync log: 7.5. A friend's review report (gist willcl-ark/9fe44851f113830b361e6a1ebbddf709) (M): 4.11.
- Dice, "Malthusian Locks" (arXiv 1511.06035), and Karlin et al. on competitive spinning: a lock that always blocks does poorly for short holds, which is why the shard lock retried before it blocked; T3 removed the retries on 22 November after they measured as no gain here (5.2).
- BatchIt (ISMM 2024), the snmalloc and mimalloc papers, the glibc documentation: a free on a thread other than the allocating one is the hard case, and the answer is to hand objects back to their owner in batches (4.3).
- Lozi et al., "The Linux Scheduler: a Decade of Wasted Cores", and Gregg, "CPU Utilization is Wrong": read waiting for a core and sleeping apart from the on-CPU share (6.1).
- Ruben Somsen, "Out-of-Order Validation & The Single Spend Check" (gist RubenSomsen/21c45b1c57448881ce5007d884fb3f21) (M): read against the series in 7.6; its double-spend handling matches the store's conflict and retry rule, its single spend check needs a store that keeps spent outputs.
- Compared and not taken (7.6): UHS (Cory Fields, bitcoin-dev May 2018) (M), SwiftSync (gist RubenSomsen/a61a37d14182ccd78760e477c78133cd, Core PR 34004), Utreexo (M), Core issue 36476, DORA and Seastar (threads bound to data, the shape of the independent design).
- The survey of other implementations (5.4), all read by subagents, figures self-reported unless said: Lopp, "2025 Bitcoin Node Performance Tests" (blog.lopp.net; six cores, every script checked to height 928,000: Core 30.0 12 h 07, libbitcoin 4.0.0 20 h 51, Gocoin out of memory at 32 GiB, Mako and btcd days; the one independent comparison found); libbitcoin-node `chaser_download.cpp`, `chaser_validate_batch.cpp`, `block_arena.cpp`; Gocoin `client/network/data.go`, `lib/utxo`; rbitcoin at 94337954 (`confirm_run/scripts.rs`, `ibd/assign.rs`, `docs/ibd-memory.md`, `docs/external_findings`); the BSV node's `txn_grouper.cpp`, `DBCacheAllInputs()` and slow-fetch options; BU's `requestManager.cpp` and `ConnectBlockCanonicalOrdering()`; Flowee's `BlockValidation.cpp`; Teranode's `validOrderAndBlessed`; the BCHN bulletin of 2021-10-31 (LMDB); Zebra's RFCs 0002 and 0004 and its batch verifiers; reth's stages and prewarming, Erigon's ETL; Firedancer's tiles; Core 29491, 36000, 35195 and secp256k1 1134, 1789.
